which allows straightforward parallelization of an entire renderer. When
installing Intel® ISPC, make sure to download an Intel® ISPC version
from \href{https://ispc.github.io/downloads.html}{ispc.github.io} that
is compatible with your Visual Studio version. After installation, put
the path to \texttt{ispc.exe} permanently into your \texttt{PATH}
environment variable or you need to correctly set the
\texttt{EMBREE\_ISPC\_EXECUTABLE} variable during CMake configuration to
point to the ISPC executable. If you want to use Intel® ISPC, you have
to enable \texttt{EMBREE\_ISPC\_SUPPORT} in CMake.

You additionally have to install
\href{http://www.cmake.org/download/}{CMake} (version 3.1 or higher).
Note that you need a native Windows CMake installation because CMake
under Cygwin cannot generate solution files for Visual Studio.

\subsection{Using the IDE}\label{using-the-ide}

Run \texttt{cmake-gui}, browse to the Embree sources, set the build
directory and click Configure. Now you can select the Generator,
e.g.~``Visual Studio 12 2013'' for a 32-bit build or ``Visual Studio 12
2013 Win64'' for a 64-bit build.

To use a different compiler than the Microsoft Visual C++ compiler, you
additionally need to specify the proper compiler toolset through the
option ``Optional toolset to use (-T parameter)''. E.g. to use Clang for
compilation set the toolset to ``LLVM\_v142''.

Do not change the toolset manually in a solution file (neither through
the project properties dialog nor through the ``Use Intel Compiler''
project context menu), because then some compiler-specific command line
options cannot be set by CMake.

Most configuration parameters described in the
\hyperref[cmake-configuration]{CMake Configuration} can be set under
Windows as well. Finally, click ``Generate'' to create the Visual Studio
solution files.

The following CMake options are only available under Windows:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{CMAKE\_CONFIGURATION\_TYPE}: List of generated configurations.
  The default value is Debug;Release;RelWithDebInfo.
\item
  \texttt{USE\_STATIC\_RUNTIME}: Use the static version of the C/C++
  runtime library. This option is turned OFF by default.
\end{itemize}

Use the generated Visual Studio solution file \texttt{embree4.sln} to
compile the project.

We recommend enabling syntax highlighting for the \texttt{.ispc} source
and \texttt{.isph} header files. To do so open Visual Studio, go to
Tools ⇒ Options ⇒ Text Editor ⇒ File Extension and add the \texttt{isph}
and \texttt{ispc} extensions for the ``Microsoft Visual C++'' editor.

\subsection{Using the Command Line}\label{using-the-command-line}

Embree can also be configured and built without the IDE using the Visual
Studio command prompt:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cd path\textbackslash{}to\textbackslash{}embree}
\NormalTok{mkdir build}
\NormalTok{cd build}
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{G }\StringTok{"Visual Studio 16 2019"} \OperatorTok{..}
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{build }\OperatorTok{.} \OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{config Release}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can also build only some projects with the \texttt{-\/-target}
switch. Additional parameters after ``\texttt{-\/-}'' will be passed to
\texttt{msbuild}. For example, to build the Embree library in parallel
use

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{build }\OperatorTok{.} \OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{config Release }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{target embree }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{m}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Building Embree - Using
vcpkg}\label{building-embree---using-vcpkg}

You can download and install Embree using the
\href{https://github.com/Microsoft/vcpkg}{vcpkg} dependency manager:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{git clone https}\OperatorTok{:}\CommentTok{//github.com/Microsoft/vcpkg.git}
\NormalTok{cd vcpkg}
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{bootstrap}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{vcpkg}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{sh}
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{vcpkg integrate install}
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{vcpkg install embree3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The Embree port in vcpkg is kept up to date by Microsoft team members
and community contributors. If the version is out of date, please
\href{https://github.com/Microsoft/vcpkg}{create an issue or pull
request} on the vcpkg repository.

\section{Windows SYCL Compilation}\label{windows-sycl-compilation}

There are two options to compile Embree with SYCL support: The open
source \href{https://github.com/intel/llvm/}{``oneAPI DPC++ Compiler''}
or the
\href{https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/developer/articles/tool/oneapi-standalone-components.html\#dpcpp-cpp}{``Intel(R)
oneAPI DPC++/C++ Compiler''}. Other SYCL compilers are not supported.
You will also need an installed version of Visual Studio that supports
the C++17 standard, e.g.~Visual Studio 2019.

The ``oneAPI DPC++ Compiler'' is more up-to-date than the ``Intel(R)
oneAPI DPC++/C++ Compiler'' but less stable. The current tested version
of the oneAPI DPC++ compiler is

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \href{https://github.com/intel/llvm/releases/download/sycl-nightly\%2F20230417/dpcpp-compiler-win.tar.gz}{oneAPI
  DPC++ Compiler 2023-04-17}
\end{itemize}

Download and unpack the archive and open the ``x64 Native Tools Command
Prompt'' of Visual Studio and execute the following lines to properly
configure the environment to use the oneAPI DPC++ compiler:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{set }\StringTok{"DPCPP\_DIR=path\_to\_dpcpp\_compiler"}
\NormalTok{set }\StringTok{"PATH=\%DPCPP\_DIR\%}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}b}\StringTok{in;\%PATH\%"}
\NormalTok{set }\StringTok{"PATH=\%DPCPP\_DIR\%}\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}l}\StringTok{ib;\%PATH\%"}
\NormalTok{set }\StringTok{"CPATH=\%DPCPP\_DIR\%}\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}i}\StringTok{nclude;\%CPATH\%"}
\NormalTok{set }\StringTok{"INCLUDE=\%DPCPP\_DIR\%}\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}i}\StringTok{nclude;\%INCLUDE\%"}
\NormalTok{set }\StringTok{"LIB=\%DPCPP\_DIR\%}\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}l}\StringTok{ib;\%LIB\%"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{path\_to\_dpcpp\_compiler} should point to the unpacked
oneAPI DPC++ compiler.

Now, you can configure Embree using CMake by executing the following
command in the Embree root directory:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{B build}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{G Ninja}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D CMAKE\_BUILD\_TYPE}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{Release}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D CMAKE\_CXX\_COMPILER}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{clang}\OperatorTok{++}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D CMAKE\_C\_COMPILER}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{clang}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D EMBREE\_SYCL\_SUPPORT}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ON}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D TBB\_ROOT}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{path\_to\_tbb\textbackslash{}lib\textbackslash{}cmake\textbackslash{}tbb}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This will create a directory \texttt{build} to use as the CMake build
directory, and configure a release build that uses \texttt{clang++} and
\texttt{clang} from the oneAPI DPC++ compiler.

The \href{https://ninja-build.org/}{Ninja} generator is currently the
easiest way to use the oneAPI DPC++ compiler.

We also enable SYCL support in Embree using the
\texttt{EMBREE\_SYCL\_SUPPORT} CMake option.

Alternatively, you can download and run the installer of the

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \href{https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/developer/articles/tool/oneapi-standalone-components.html\#dpcpp-cpp}{Intel(R)
  oneAPI DPC++/C++ Compiler 2023.0.0}.
\end{itemize}

After installation, you can either open a regular
\texttt{Command\ Prompt} and execute the \texttt{vars.bat} script in the
\texttt{env} directory of the compiler install directory, for example

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{C}\OperatorTok{:}\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}Program Files }\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{x86}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}Intel\textbackslash{}oneAPI\textbackslash{}compiler\textbackslash{}}\FloatTok{2023.0}\ErrorTok{.0}\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}env\textbackslash{}vars}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{bat}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

or simply open the installed ``Intel oneAPI command prompt for Intel 64
for Visual Studio''.

Both ways will put the \texttt{icx} compiler executable from the
Intel(R) oneAPI DPC++/C++ compiler in your path.

Now, you can configure Embree using CMake by executing the following
command in the Embree root directory:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{B build}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{G Ninja}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D CMAKE\_BUILD\_TYPE}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{Release}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D CMAKE\_CXX\_COMPILER}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{icx}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D CMAKE\_C\_COMPILER}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{icx}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D EMBREE\_SYCL\_SUPPORT}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ON}
      \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{D TBB\_ROOT}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{path\_to\_tbb\textbackslash{}lib\textbackslash{}cmake\textbackslash{}tbb}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

More information about setting up the Intel(R) oneAPI DPC++/C++ compiler
can be found in the
\href{https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/develop/documentation/oneapi-dpcpp-cpp-compiler-dev-guide-and-reference/top/compiler-setup.html}{Development
Reference Guide}. Please note, that the Intel(R) oneAPI DPC++/C++
compiler requires
\href{https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/develop/documentation/oneapi-dpcpp-cpp-compiler-dev-guide-and-reference/top/compiler-setup/use-the-command-line/use-cmake-with-the-compiler.html}{at
least CMake version 3.23 on Windows}.

Independent of the DPC++ compiler choice, you can now build Embree using

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{build build}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you have problems with Ninja re-running CMake in an infinite loop,
then first remove the ``Re-run CMake if any of its inputs changed.''
section from the \texttt{build.ninja} file and run the above command
again.

You can also create an Embree package using the following command:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{cmake }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{build build }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{target package}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Please see the \hyperref[building-embree-sycl-applications]{Building
Embree SYCL Applications} section on how to build your application with
such an Embree package.

\subsection{Windows Graphics Driver
Installation}\label{windows-graphics-driver-installation}

In order to run the SYCL tutorials on HPG hardware, you first need to
install the graphics drivers for your graphics card from
\url{https://www.intel.com}. Please make sure to have installed version
31.0.101.4314 or newer.

\section{CMake Configuration}\label{cmake-configuration}

The default CMake configuration in the configuration dialog should be
appropriate for most usages. The following list describes all parameters
that can be configured in CMake:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{CMAKE\_BUILD\_TYPE}: Can be used to switch between Debug mode
  (Debug), Release mode (Release) (default), and Release mode with
  enabled assertions and debug symbols (RelWithDebInfo).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_STACK\_PROTECTOR}: Enables protection of return
  address from buffer overwrites. This option is OFF by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_ISPC\_SUPPORT}: Enables Intel® ISPC support of Embree.
  This option is OFF by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_SYCL\_SUPPORT}: Enables GPU support using SYCL. When
  this option is enabled you have to use some DPC++ compiler. Please see
  the sections \hyperref[linux-sycl-compilation]{Linux SYCL Compilation}
  and \hyperref[windows-sycl-compilation]{Windows SYCL Compilation} on
  supported DPC++ compilers. This option is OFF by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_SYCL\_AOT\_DEVICES}: Selects a list of GPU devices for
  ahead-of-time (AOT) compilation of device code. Possible values are
  either, ``none'' which enables only just in time (JIT) compilation, or
  a list of the Embree-supported Xe GPUs for AOT compilation:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    XE\_HPG\_CORE : Xe HPG devices
  \item
    XE\_HPC\_CORE : Xe HPC devices
  \end{itemize}

  One can also specify multiple devices separated by comma to compile
  ahead of time for multiple devices,
  e.g.~``XE\_HPG\_CORE,XE\_HP\_CORE''. When enabling AOT compilation for
  one or multiple devices, JIT compilation will always additionally be
  enabled in case the code is executed on a device no code is
  precompiled for.

  Execute ``ocloc compile --help'' for more details of possible devices
  to pass. Embree is only supported on Xe HPG/HPC and newer devices.

  Per default, this option is set to ``none'' to enable JIT compilation.
  We recommend using JIT compilation as this enables the use of
  specialization constants to reduce code complexity.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_STATIC\_LIB}: Builds Embree as a static library (OFF
  by default). Further multiple static libraries are generated for the
  different ISAs selected (e.g.~\texttt{embree4.a},
  \texttt{embree4\_sse42.a}, \texttt{embree4\_avx.a},
  \texttt{embree4\_avx2.a}, \texttt{embree4\_avx512.a}). You have to
  link these libraries in exactly this order of increasing ISA.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_API\_NAMESPACE}: Specifies a namespace name to put all
  Embree API symbols inside. By default, no namespace is used and plain
  C symbols are exported.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_LIBRARY\_NAME}: Specifies the name of the Embree
  library file created. By default, the name embree4 is used.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_IGNORE\_CMAKE\_CXX\_FLAGS}: When enabled, Embree
  ignores default CMAKE\_CXX\_FLAGS. This option is turned ON by
  default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TUTORIALS}: Enables build of Embree tutorials (default
  ON).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_BACKFACE\_CULLING}: Enables backface culling,
  i.e.~only surfaces facing a ray can be hit. This option is turned OFF
  by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_CURVES}: Enables backface culling
  for curves, i.e.~only surfaces facing a ray can be hit. This option is
  turned OFF by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_SPHERES}: Enables backface culling
  for spheres, i.e.~only surfaces facing a ray can be hit. This option
  is turned OFF by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_COMPACT\_POLYS}: Enables compact tris/quads, i.e.~only
  geomIDs and primIDs are stored inside the leaf nodes.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_FILTER\_FUNCTION}: Enables the intersection filter
  function feature (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_RAY\_MASK}: Enables the ray masking feature (OFF by
  default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_RAY\_PACKETS}: Enables ray packet traversal kernels.
  This feature is turned ON by default. When turned on packet traversal
  is used internally and packets passed to rtcIntersect4/8/16 are kept
  intact in callbacks (when the ISA of appropriate width is enabled).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_IGNORE\_INVALID\_RAYS}: Makes code robust against the
  risk of full-tree traversals caused by invalid rays (e.g.~rays
  containing INF/NaN as origins). This option is turned OFF by default.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TASKING\_SYSTEM}: Chooses between Intel® Threading TBB
  Building Blocks (TBB), Parallel Patterns Library (PPL) (Windows only),
  or an internal tasking system (INTERNAL). By default, TBB is used.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TBB\_ROOT}: If Intel® Threading Building Blocks (TBB)
  is used as a tasking system, search the library in this directory
  tree.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TBB\_COMPONENT}: The component/library name of Intel®
  Threading Building Blocks (TBB). Embree searches for this library name
  (default: tbb) when TBB is used as the tasking system.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TBB\_POSTFIX}: If Intel® Threading Building Blocks
  (TBB) is used as a tasking system, link to tbb.(so,dll,lib). Defaults
  to the empty string.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TBB\_DEBUG\_ROOT}: If Intel® Threading Building Blocks
  (TBB) is used as a tasking system, search the library in this
  directory tree in Debug mode. Defaults to \texttt{EMBREE\_TBB\_ROOT}.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_TBB\_DEBUG\_POSTFIX}: If Intel® Threading Building
  Blocks (TBB) is used as a tasking system, link to tbb.(so,dll,lib) in
  Debug mode. Defaults to ``\_debug''.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_MAX\_ISA}: Select highest supported ISA (SSE2, SSE4.2,
  AVX, AVX2, AVX512, or NONE). When set to NONE the EMBREE\_ISA\_*
  variables can be used to enable ISAs individually. By default, the
  option is set to AVX2.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_ISA\_SSE2}: Enables SSE2 when EMBREE\_MAX\_ISA is set
  to NONE. By default, this option is turned OFF.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_ISA\_SSE42}: Enables SSE4.2 when EMBREE\_MAX\_ISA is
  set to NONE. By default, this option is turned OFF.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_ISA\_AVX}: Enables AVX when EMBREE\_MAX\_ISA is set to
  NONE. By default, this option is turned OFF.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_ISA\_AVX2}: Enables AVX2 when EMBREE\_MAX\_ISA is set
  to NONE. By default, this option is turned OFF.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_ISA\_AVX512}: Enables AVX-512 for Skylake when
  EMBREE\_MAX\_ISA is set to NONE. By default, this option is turned
  OFF.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_TRIANGLE}: Enables support for triangle
  geometries (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_QUAD}: Enables support for quad geometries
  (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_CURVE}: Enables support for curve geometries
  (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_SUBDIVISION}: Enables support for
  subdivision geometries (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_INSTANCE}: Enables support for instances (ON
  by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_USER}: Enables support for user-defined
  geometries (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_POINT}: Enables support for point geometries
  (ON by default).
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_CURVE\_SELF\_INTERSECTION\_AVOIDANCE\_FACTOR}:
  Specifies a factor that controls the self-intersection avoidance
  feature for flat curves. Flat curve intersections which are closer
  than
  curve\_radius*\texttt{EMBREE\_CURVE\_SELF\_INTERSECTION\_AVOIDANCE\_FACTOR}
  to the ray origin are ignored. A value of 0.0f disables
  self-intersection avoidance while 2.0f is the default value.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_DISC\_POINT\_SELF\_INTERSECTION\_AVOIDANCE}: Enables
  self-intersection avoidance for RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_DISC\_POINT
  geometry type (ON by default). When enabled intersections are skipped
  if the ray origin lies inside the sphere defined by the point
  primitive.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_MIN\_WIDTH}: Enabled the min-width feature, which
  allows increasing the radius of curves and points to match some amount
  of pixels. See
  \hyperref[rtcsetgeometrymaxradiusscale]{rtcSetGeometryMaxRadiusScale}
  for more details.
\item
  \texttt{EMBREE\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT}: Specifies the maximum
  number of nested instance levels. Should be greater than 0; the
  default value is 1. Instances nested any deeper than this value will
  silently disappear in release mode, and cause assertions in debug
  mode.
\end{itemize}

\pagebreak

\chapter{Embree API}\label{embree-api}

The Embree API is a low-level C99 ray tracing API which can be used to
build spatial index structures for 3D scenes and perform ray queries of
different types.

The API can get used on the CPU using standard C, C++, and ISPC code and
Intel GPUs by using SYCL code.

The Intel® Implicit SPMD Program Compiler (Intel® ISPC) version of the
API, is almost identical to the standard C99 version, but contains
additional functions that operate on ray packets with a size of the
native SIMD width used by Intel® ISPC.

The SYCL version of the API is also mostly identical to the C99 version
of the API, with some exceptions listed in section
\hyperref[embree-sycl-api]{Embree SYCL API}.

For simplicity this document refers to the C99 version of the API
functions. For changes when upgrading from the Embree 3 to the current
Embree 4 API see Section
\hyperref[upgrading-from-embree-3-to-embree-4]{Upgrading from Embree 3
to Embree 4}.

All API calls carry the prefix \texttt{rtc} (or \texttt{RTC} for types)
which stands for \textbf{r}ay \textbf{t}racing \textbf{c}ore. The API
supports scenes consisting of different geometry types such as triangle
meshes, quad meshes (triangle pairs), grid meshes, flat curves, round
curves, oriented curves, subdivision meshes, instances, and user-defined
geometries. See Section \hyperref[scene-object]{Scene Object} for more
information.

Finding the closest hit of a ray segment with the scene
(\texttt{rtcIntersect}-type functions), and determining whether any hit
between a ray segment and the scene exists (\texttt{rtcOccluded}-type
functions) are both supported. The API supports queries for single rays
and ray packets. See Section \hyperref[ray-queries]{Ray Queries} for
more information.

The API is designed in an object-oriented manner, e.g.~it contains
device objects (\texttt{RTCDevice} type), scene objects
(\texttt{RTCScene} type), geometry objects (\texttt{RTCGeometry} type),
buffer objects (\texttt{RTCBuffer} type), and BVH objects
(\texttt{RTCBVH} type). All objects are reference counted, and handles
can be released by calling the appropriate release function
(e.g.~\texttt{rtcReleaseDevice}) or retained by incrementing the
reference count (e.g.~\texttt{rtcRetainDevice}). In general, API calls
that access the same object are not thread-safe, unless specified
otherwise. However, attaching geometries to the same scene and
performing ray queries in a scene is thread-safe.

\section{Device Object}\label{device-object}

Embree supports a device concept, which allows different components of
the application to use the Embree API without interfering with each
other. An application typically first creates a device using the
\hyperref[rtcnewdevice]{rtcNewDevice} function (or
\hyperref[rtcnewsycldevice]{rtcNewSYCLDevice} when using SYCL for the
GPU). This device can then be used to construct further objects, such as
scenes and geometries. Before the application exits, it should release
all devices by invoking \hyperref[rtcreleasedevice]{rtcReleaseDevice}.
An application typically creates only a single device. If required
differently, it should only use a small number of devices at any given
time.

Each user thread has its own error flag per device. If an error occurs
when invoking an API function, this flag is set to an error code (if it
isn't already set by a previous error). See Section
\hyperref[rtcgetdeviceerror]{rtcGetDeviceError} for information on how
to read the error code and Section
\hyperref[rtcsetdeviceerrorfunction]{rtcSetDeviceErrorFunction} on how
to register a callback that is invoked for each error encountered. It is
recommended to always set a error callback function, to detect all
errors.

\section{Scene Object}\label{scene-object}

A scene is a container for a set of geometries, and contains a spatial
acceleration structure which can be used to perform different types of
ray queries.

A scene is created using the \texttt{rtcNewScene} function call, and
released using the \texttt{rtcReleaseScene} function call. To populate a
scene with geometries use the \texttt{rtcAttachGeometry} call, and to
detach them use the \texttt{rtcDetachGeometry} call. Once all scene
geometries are attached, an \texttt{rtcCommitScene} call (or
\texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} call) will finish the scene description and
trigger building of internal data structures. After the scene got
committed, it is safe to perform ray queries (see Section
\hyperref[ray-queries]{Ray Queries}) or to query the scene bounding box
(see \hyperref[rtcgetscenebounds]{rtcGetSceneBounds} and
\hyperref[rtcgetscenelinearbounds]{rtcGetSceneLinearBounds}).

If scene geometries get modified or attached or detached, the
\texttt{rtcCommitScene} call must be invoked before performing any
further ray queries for the scene; otherwise the effect of the ray query
is undefined. The modification of a geometry, committing the scene, and
tracing of rays must always happen sequentially, and never at the same
time. Any API call that sets a property of the scene or geometries
contained in the scene count as scene modification, e.g.~including
setting of intersection filter functions.

Scene flags can be used to configure a scene to use less memory
(\texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_COMPACT}), use more robust traversal
algorithms (\texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_ROBUST}), and to optimize for
dynamic content. See Section
\hyperref[rtcsetsceneflags]{rtcSetSceneFlags} for more details.

A build quality can be specified for a scene to balance between
acceleration structure build performance and ray query performance. See
Section \hyperref[rtcsetscenebuildquality]{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality} for
more details on build quality.

\section{Geometry Object}\label{geometry-object}

A new geometry is created using the \texttt{rtcNewGeometry} function.
Depending on the geometry type, different buffers must be bound (e.g.
using \texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer}) to set up the geometry data.
In most cases, binding of a vertex and index buffer is required. The
number of primitives and vertices of that geometry is typically inferred
from the size of these bound buffers.

Changes to the geometry always must be committed using the
\texttt{rtcCommitGeometry} call before using the geometry. After
committing, a geometry is not included in any scene. A geometry can be
added to a scene by using the \texttt{rtcAttachGeometry} function (to
automatically assign a geometry ID) or using the
\texttt{rtcAttachGeometryById} function (to specify the geometry ID
manually). A geometry can get attached to multiple scenes.

All geometry types support multi-segment motion blur with an arbitrary
number of equidistant time steps (in the range of 2 to 129) inside a
user specified time range. Each geometry can have a different number of
time steps and a different time range. The motion blur geometry is
defined by linearly interpolating the geometries of neighboring time
steps. To construct a motion blur geometry, first the number of time
steps of the geometry must be specified using the
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeStepCount} function, and then a vertex buffer
for each time step must be bound, e.g.~using the
\texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer} function. Optionally, a time range
defining the start (and end time) of the first (and last) time step can
be set using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeRange} function. This feature
will also allow geometries to appear and disappear during the camera
shutter time if the time range is a sub range of {[}0,1{]}.

\section{Ray Queries}\label{ray-queries}

The API supports finding the closest hit of a ray segment with the scene
(\texttt{rtcIntersect}-type functions), and determining whether any hit
between a ray segment and the scene exists (\texttt{rtcOccluded}-type
functions).

Supported are single ray queries (\texttt{rtcIntersect1} and
\texttt{rtcOccluded1}) as well as ray packet queries for ray packets of
size 4 (\texttt{rtcIntersect4} and \texttt{rtcOccluded4}), ray packets
of size 8 (\texttt{rtcIntersect8} and \texttt{rtcOccluded8}), and ray
packets of size 16 (\texttt{rtcIntersect16} and \texttt{rtcOccluded16}).

See Sections \hyperref[rtcintersect1]{rtcIntersect1} and
\hyperref[rtcoccluded1]{rtcOccluded1} for a detailed description of how
to set up and trace a ray.

See tutorial \hyperref[triangle-geometry]{Triangle Geometry} for a
complete example of how to trace single rays and ray packets.

\section{Point Queries}\label{point-queries}

The API supports traversal of the BVH using a point query object that
specifies a location and a query radius. For all primitives intersecting
the according domain, a user defined callback function is called which
allows queries such as finding the closest point on the surface
geometries of the scene (see Tutorial \hyperref[closest-point]{Closest
Point}) or nearest neighbour queries (see Tutorial
\hyperref[voronoi]{Voronoi}).

See Section \hyperref[rtcpointquery]{rtcPointQuery} for a detailed
description of how to set up point queries.

\section{Collision Detection}\label{collision-detection}

The Embree API also supports collision detection queries between two
scenes consisting only of user geometries. Embree only performs
broadphase collision detection, the narrow phase detection can be
performed through a callback function.

See Section \hyperref[rtccollide]{rtcCollide} for a detailed description
of how to set up collision detection.

Seen tutorial \hyperref[collision-detection]{Collision Detection} for a
complete example of collision detection being used on a simple cloth
solver.

\section{Filter Functions}\label{filter-functions}

The API supports filter functions that are invoked for each intersection
found during the \texttt{rtcIntersect}-type or \texttt{rtcOccluded}-type
calls.

The filter functions can be set per-geometry using the
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryIntersectFilterFunction} and
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryOccludedFilterFunction} calls. The former ones are
called geometry intersection filter functions, the latter ones geometry
occlusion filter functions. These filter functions are designed to be
used to ignore intersections outside of a user-defined silhouette of a
primitive, e.g.~to model tree leaves using transparency textures.

The filter function can also get passed as arguments directly to the
traversal functions, see section
\hyperref[rtcinitintersectarguments]{rtcInitIntersectArguments} and
\hyperref[rtcinitoccludedarguments]{rtcInitOccludedArguments} for more
details. These argument filter functions are designed to change the
semantics of the ray query, e.g.~to accumulate opacity for transparent
shadows, count the number of surfaces along a ray, collect all hits
along a ray, etc. The argument filter function must be enabled to be
used for a scene using the
\texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_FILTER\_FUNCTION\_IN\_ARGUMENTS} scene flag.
The callback is only invoked for geometries that enable the callback
using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryEnableFilterFunctionFromArguments} call,
or enabled for all geometries when the
\texttt{RTC\_RAY\_QUERY\_FLAG\_INVOKE\_ARGUMENT\_FILTER} ray query flag
is set.

\section{BVH Build API}\label{bvh-build-api}

The internal algorithms to build a BVH are exposed through the
\texttt{RTCBVH} object and \texttt{rtcBuildBVH} call. This call makes it
possible to build a BVH in a user-specified format over user-specified
primitives. See the documentation of the \texttt{rtcBuildBVH} call for
more details.

\chapter{Embree SYCL API}\label{embree-sycl-api}

Embree supports ray tracing on Intel GPUs by using the SYCL programming
language. SYCL is a Khronos standardized C++ based language for single
source heterogenous programming for acceleration offload, see the
\href{https://www.khronos.org/sycl/}{SYCL webpage} for details.

The Embree SYCL API is designed for photorealistic rendering use cases,
where scene setup is performed on the host, and rendering on the device.
The Embree SYCL API is very similar to the standard Embree C99 API, and
supports most of its features, such as all triangle-type geometries, all
curve types and basis functions, point geometry types, user geometries,
filter callbacks, multi-level instancing, and motion blur.

To enable SYCL support you have to include the \texttt{sycl.hpp} file
before the Embree API headers:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}sycl/sycl.hpp\textgreater{}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Next you need to initializes an Embree SYCL device using the
\texttt{rtcNewSYCLDevice} API function by providing a SYCL context.

Embree provides the \texttt{rtcIsSYCLDeviceSupported} API function to
check if some SYCL device is supported by Embree. You can also use the
\texttt{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector} to conveniently select the first SYCL
device that is supported by Embree, e.g.:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device device}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{queue queue}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ exception\_handler}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{context context}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{RTCDevice device }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rtcNewSYCLDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,}\StringTok{""}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Scenes created with an Embree SYCL device can only get used to trace
rays using SYCL on the GPU, it is not possible to trace rays on the CPU
with such a device. To render on the CPU and GPU in parallel, the user
has to create a second Embree device and create a second scene to be
used on the CPU.

Files containing SYCL code, have to get compiled with the Intel® oneAPI
DPC++ compiler. Please see section
\hyperref[linux-sycl-compilation]{Linux SYCL Compilation} and
\hyperref[windows-sycl-compilation]{Windows SYCL Compilation} for
supported compilers. The DPC++ compiler performs a two-phase
compilation, where host code is compiled in a first phase, and device
code compiled in a second compilation phase.

Standard Embree API functions for scene construction can get used on the
host but not the device. Data buffers that are shared with Embree
(e.g.~for vertex of index buffers) have to get allocated as SYCL unified
shared memory (USM memory), using the \texttt{sycl::malloc} or
\texttt{sycl::aligned\_alloc} calls with
\texttt{sycl::usm::alloc::shared} property, or the
sycl::aligned\_alloc\_shared call, e.g:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ptr }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{aligned\_alloc}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ bytes}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ queue}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{usm}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{alloc}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{shared}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

These shared allocations have to be valid during rendering, as Embree
may access contained data when tracing rays. Embree does not support
device-only memory allocations, as the BVH builder implemented on the
CPU relies on reading the data buffers.

Device side rendering can get invoked by submitting a SYCL
\texttt{parallel\_for} to the SYCL queue:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\AttributeTok{const}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{specialization\_id}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{RTCFeatureFlags}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ feature\_mask}\OperatorTok{;}

\NormalTok{RTCFeatureFlags required\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ RTC\_FEATURE\_FLAG\_TRIANGLE}\OperatorTok{;}

\NormalTok{queue}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{submit}\OperatorTok{([=](}\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{handler}\OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{ cgh}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  cgh}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{set\_specialization\_constant}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{feature\_mask}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}(}\NormalTok{required\_features}\OperatorTok{);}
  
\NormalTok{  cgh}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{parallel\_for}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{range}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{),[=](}\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{id}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ item}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{kernel\_handler kh}\OperatorTok{)}
  \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    RTCIntersectArguments args}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rtcInitIntersectArguments}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{args}\OperatorTok{);}

    \AttributeTok{const}\NormalTok{ RTCFeatureFlags features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ kh}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{get\_specialization\_constant}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{feature\_mask}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}();}
\NormalTok{    args}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{feature\_mask }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ features}\OperatorTok{;}

    \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCRayHit rayhit}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{org\_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ox}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{org\_y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ oy}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{org\_z }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ oz}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{dir\_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dx}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{dir\_y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dy}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{dir\_z }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dz}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{tnear }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{tfar }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{std::}\NormalTok{numeric\_limits}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}::}\NormalTok{infinity}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{mask }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{flags }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{hit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{geomID }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ RTC\_INVALID\_GEOMETRY\_ID}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{hit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{instID}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ RTC\_INVALID\_GEOMETRY\_ID}\OperatorTok{;}

\NormalTok{    rtcIntersect1}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{scene}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{rayhit}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{args}\OperatorTok{);}

\NormalTok{    result}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{geomID }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{hit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{geomID}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    result}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{primID }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{hit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{primID}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{    result}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tfar }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rayhit}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{tfar}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\});}
\OperatorTok{\});}
\NormalTok{queue}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{wait\_and\_throw}\OperatorTok{();}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This example passes a feature mask using a specialization contant to the
\texttt{rtcIntersect1} function, which is recommended for GPU rendering.
For best performance, this feature mask should get used to enable only
features required by the application to render the scene, e.g.~just
triangles in this example.

Inside the SYCL \texttt{parallel\_for} loop you can use rendering
related functions, such as the \texttt{rtcIntersect1} and
\texttt{rtcOccluded1} functions to trace rays,
\texttt{rtcForwardIntersect1} and \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded1} to
continue object traversal from inside a user geometry callback, and
\texttt{rtcGetGeometryUserDataFromScene} to get the user data pointer of
some geometry.

Have a look at the \hyperref[minimal]{Minimal} tutorial for a minimal
SYCL example.

\section{SYCL JIT caching}\label{sycl-jit-caching}

Compile times for just in time compilation (JIT compilation) can be
large. To resolve this issue we recommend enabling persistent JIT
compilation caching inside your application, by setting the
\texttt{SYCL\_CACHE\_PERSISTENT} environment variable to \texttt{1}, and
the \texttt{SYCL\_CACHE\_DIR} environment variable to some proper
directory where the JIT cache should get stored. These environment
variables have to get set before the SYCL device is created, e.g:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{setenv}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"SYCL\_CACHE\_PERSISTENT"}\OperatorTok{,}\StringTok{"1"}\OperatorTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{setenv}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"SYCL\_CACHE\_DIR"}\OperatorTok{,}\StringTok{"cache\_dir"}\OperatorTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{);}

\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device device}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{SYCL Memory Pooling}\label{sycl-memory-pooling}

Memory Pooling is a mechanism where small USM memory allocations are
packed into larger allocation blocks. This mode is required when your
application performs many small USM allocations, as otherwise only a
small fraction of GPU memory is usable and data transfer performance
will be low.

Memory pooling is supported for USM allocations that are read-only by
the device. The following example allocated device read-only memory with
memory pooling support:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{aligned\_alloc\_shared}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{align}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ bytes}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ queue}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{ext}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{oneapi}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{property}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{usm}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device\_read\_only}\OperatorTok{());}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Embree SYCL Limitations}\label{embree-sycl-limitations}

Embree only supports Xe HPC and HPG GPUs as SYCL devices, thus in
particular the CPU and other GPUs cannot get used as a SYCL device. To
render on the CPU just use the standard C99 API without relying on SYCL.

The SYCL language spec puts some restrictions to device functions, such
as disallowing: global variable access, malloc, invokation of virtual
functions, function pointers, runtime type information, exceptions,
recursion, etc. See Section
\texttt{5.4.\ Language\ Restrictions\ for\ device\ functions} of the
\href{https://www.khronos.org/registry/SYCL/specs/sycl-2020/html/sycl-2020.html\#sec:language.restrictions.kernels}{SYCL
specification} for more details.

Using Intel's oneAPI DPC++ compiler invoking an indirectly called
function is allowed, but we do not recommend this for performance
reasons.

Some features are not supported by the Embree SYCL API thus cannot get
used on the GPU:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The packet tracing functions \texttt{rtcIntersect4/8/16} and
  \texttt{rtcOccluded4/8/16} are not supported in SYCL device side code.
  Using these functions makes no sense for SYCL, as the programming
  model is implicitely executed in SIMT mode on the GPU anyway.
\item
  Filter and user geometry callbacks stored inside the geometry objects
  are not supported on SYCL. Please use the alternative approach of
  passing the function pointer through the
  \texttt{RTCIntersectArguments} (or \texttt{RTCOccludedArguments})
  structures to the tracing function, which enables inlining on the GPU.
\item
  The \texttt{rtcInterpolate} function cannot get used on the the
  device. For most primitive types the vertex data interpolation is
  anyway a trivial operation, and an API call just introduces overheads.
  On the CPU that overhead is acceptable, but on the GPU it is not. The
  \texttt{rtcInterpolate} function does not know the geometry type it is
  interpolating over, thus its implementation on the GPU would contain a
  large switch statement for all potential geometry types.
\item
  Tracing rays using \texttt{rtcIntersect1} and \texttt{rtcOccluded1}
  functions from user geometry callbacks is not supported in SYCL.
  Please use the tail recursive \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect1} and
  \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded1} calls instead.
\item
  Subdivision surfaces are not supported for Embree SYCL devices.
\item
  Collision detection (\texttt{rtcCollide} API call) is not supported in
  SYCL device side code.
\item
  Point queries (\texttt{rtcPointQuery} API call) are not supported in
  SYCL device side code.
\end{itemize}

\section{Embree SYCL Known Issues}\label{embree-sycl-known-issues}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The SYCL support of Embree is in beta phase. Current functionality,
  quality, and GPU performance may not reflect that of the final
  product.
\item
  Compilation with build configuration ``debug'' is currently not
  working on Windows.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Upgrading from Embree 3 to Embree
4}\label{upgrading-from-embree-3-to-embree-4}

This section summarizes API changes between Embree 3 and Embree4. Most
of these changes are motivated by GPU performance and having a
consistent API that works properly for the CPU and GPU.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The API include folder got renamed from embree3 to embree4, to be able
  to install Embree 3 and Embree 4 side by side, without having
  conflicts in API folder.
\item
  The \texttt{RTCIntersectContext} is renamed to
  \texttt{RTCRayQueryContext} and the \texttt{RTCIntersectContextFlags}
  got renamed to \texttt{RTCRayQueryFlags}.
\item
  There are some changes to the \texttt{rtcIntersect} and
  \texttt{rtcOccluded} functions. Most members of the old intersect
  context have been moved to some optional
  \texttt{RTCIntersectArguments} (and \texttt{RTCOccludedArguments})
  structures, which also contains a pointer to the new ray query
  context. The argument structs fulfill the task of providing additional
  advanced arguments to the traversal functions. The ray query context
  can get used to pass additional data to callbacks, and to maintain an
  instID stack in case instancing is done manually inside user geometry
  callbacks. The arguments struct is not available inside callbacks.
  This change was in particular necessary for SYCL to allow inlining of
  function pointers provided to the traversal functions, and to reduce
  the amount of state passed to callbacks, which both improves GPU
  performance. Most applications can just drop passing the ray query
  context to port to Embree 4.
\item
  The \texttt{rtcFilterIntersection} and \texttt{rtcFilterOcclusion} API
  calls that invoke both, the geometry and argument version of the
  filter callback, from a user geometry callback are no longer
  supported. Instead applications should use the
  \texttt{rtcInvokeIntersectFilterFromGeometry} and
  \texttt{rtcInvokeOccludedFilterFromGeometry} API calls that invoke
  just the geometry version of the filter function, and invoke the
  argument filter function manually if required.
\item
  The filter function passed as arguments to \texttt{rtcIntersect} and
  \texttt{rtcOccluded} functions is only invoked for some geometry if
  enabled through
  \texttt{rtcSetGeometryEnableFilterFunctionFromArguments} for that
  geometry. Alternatively, argument filter functions can get enabled for
  all geometries using the
  \texttt{RTC\_RAY\_QUERY\_FLAG\_INVOKE\_ARGUMENT\_FILTER} ray query
  flag.
\item
  User geometry callbacks get a valid vector as input to identify valid
  and invalid rays. In Embree 3 the user geometry callback just had to
  update the ray hit members when an intersection was found and perform
  no operation otherwise. In Embree 4 the callback additionally has to
  return valid=-1 when a hit was found, and valid=0 when no hit was
  found. This allows Embree to properly pass the new hit distance to the
  ray tracing hardware only in the case a hit was found.
\item
  Further ray masking is enabled by default now as required by most
  applications and the default ray mask for geometries got changed from
  0xFFFFFFFF to 0x1.
\item
  The stream tracing functions \texttt{rtcIntersect1M},
  \texttt{rtcIntersect1Mp}, \texttt{rtcIntersectNM},
  \texttt{rtcIntersectNp}, \texttt{rtcOccluded1M},
  \texttt{rtcOccluded1Mp}, \texttt{rtcOccludedNM}, and
  \texttt{rtcOccludedNp} got removed as they were rarely used and did
  not provide relevant performance benefits. As alternative the
  application can just iterate over \texttt{rtcIntersect1} and
  potentially \texttt{rtcIntersect4/8/16} to get similar performance.
\end{itemize}

To use Embree through SYCL on the CPU and GPU additional changes are
required:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Embree 3 allows to use \texttt{rtcIntersect} recursively from a user
  geometry or intersection filter callback to continue a ray inside an
  instantiated object. In Embree 4 using \texttt{rtcIntersect}
  recursively is disallowed on the GPU but still supported on the CPU.
  To properly continue a ray inside an instantiated object use the new
  \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect1} and \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded1}
  functions.
\item
  The geometry object of Embree 4 is a host side only object, thus
  accessing it during rendering from the GPU is not allowed. Thus all
  API functions that take an RTCGeometry object as argument cannot get
  used during rendering. Thus in particular the
  \texttt{rtcGetGeometryUserData(RTCGeometry)} call cannot get used, but
  there is an alternative function
  \texttt{rtcGetGeometryUserDataFromScene(RTCScene\ scene,uint\ geomID)}
  that should get used instead.
\item
  The user geometry callback and filter callback functions should get
  passed through the intersection and occlusion argument structures to
  the \texttt{rtcIntersect1} and \texttt{rtcOccluded1} functions
  directly to allow inlining. The experimental geometry version of the
  callbacks is disabled in SYCL and should not get used.
\item
  The feature flags should get used in SYCL to minimal GPU code for
  optimal performance.
\item
  The \texttt{rtcInterpolate} function cannot get used on the device,
  and vertex data interpolation should get implemented by the
  application.
\item
  Indirectly called functions must be declared with
  \texttt{RTC\_SYCL\_INDIRECTLY\_CALLABLE} when used as filter or user
  geometry callbacks.
\end{itemize}

\pagebreak

\chapter{Embree API Reference}\label{embree-api-reference}

\section{rtcNewDevice}\label{rtcnewdevice}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcNewDevice }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ creates a }\KeywordTok{new}\NormalTok{ device}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCDevice rtcNewDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\AttributeTok{const} \DataTypeTok{char}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ config}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function creates a new device to be used for CPU ray tracing and
returns a handle to this device. The device object is reference counted
with an initial reference count of 1. The handle can be released using
the \texttt{rtcReleaseDevice} API call.

The device object acts as a class factory for all other object types.
All objects created from the device (like scenes, geometries, etc.) hold
a reference to the device, thus the device will not be destroyed unless
these objects are destroyed first.

Objects are only compatible if they belong to the same device, e.g it is
not allowed to create a geometry in one device and attach it to a scene
created with a different device.

A configuration string (\texttt{config} argument) can be passed to the
device construction. This configuration string can be \texttt{NULL} to
use the default configuration.

The following configuration is supported:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{threads={[}int{]}}: Specifies a number of build threads to
  use. A value of 0 enables all detected hardware threads. By default
  all hardware threads are used.
\item
  \texttt{user\_threads={[}int{]}}: Sets the number of user threads that
  can be used to join and participate in a scene commit using
  \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene}. The tasking system will only use
  threads-user\_threads many worker threads, thus if the app wants to
  solely use its threads to commit scenes, just set threads equal to
  user\_threads. This option only has effect with the Intel(R) Threading
  Building Blocks (TBB) tasking system.
\item
  \texttt{set\_affinity={[}0/1{]}}: When enabled, build threads are
  affinitized to hardware threads. This option is disabled by default on
  standard CPUs, and enabled by default on Xeon Phi Processors.
\item
  \texttt{start\_threads={[}0/1{]}}: When enabled, the build threads are
  started upfront. This can be useful for benchmarking to exclude thread
  creation time. This option is disabled by default.
\item
  \texttt{isa={[}sse2,sse4.2,avx,avx2,avx512{]}}: Use specified ISA. By
  default the ISA is selected automatically.
\item
  \texttt{max\_isa={[}sse2,sse4.2,avx,avx2,avx512{]}}: Configures the
  automated ISA selection to use maximally the specified ISA.
\item
  \texttt{hugepages={[}0/1{]}}: Enables or disables usage of huge pages.
  Under Linux huge pages are used by default but under Windows and macOS
  they are disabled by default.
\item
  \texttt{enable\_selockmemoryprivilege={[}0/1{]}}: When set to 1, this
  enables the \texttt{SeLockMemoryPrivilege} privilege with is required
  to use huge pages on Windows. This option has an effect only under
  Windows and is ignored on other platforms. See Section
  \hyperref[huge-page-support]{Huge Page Support} for more details.
\item
  \texttt{verbose={[}0,1,2,3{]}}: Sets the verbosity of the output. When
  set to 0, no output is printed by Embree, when set to a higher level
  more output is printed. By default Embree does not print anything on
  the console.
\item
  \texttt{frequency\_level={[}simd128,simd256,simd512{]}}: Specifies the
  frequency level the application want to run on, which can be either:

  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
  \tightlist
  \item
    simd128 to run at highest frequency
  \item
    simd256 to run at AVX2-heavy frequency level
  \item
    simd512 to run at heavy AVX512 frequency level. When some frequency
    level is specified, Embree will avoid doing optimizations that may
    reduce the frequency level below the level specified. E.g. if your
    app does not use AVX instructions setting
    ``frequency\_level=simd128'' will cause some CPUs to run at highest
    frequency, which may result in higher application performance if you
    do much shading. If you application heavily uses AVX code, you
    should best set the frequency level to simd256. Per default Embree
    tries to avoid reducing the frequency of the CPU by setting the
    simd256 level only when the CPU has no significant down clocking.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{itemize}

Different configuration options should be separated by commas, e.g.:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcNewDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"threads=1,isa=avx"}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On success returns a handle of the created device. On failure returns
\texttt{NULL} as device and sets a per-thread error code that can be
queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError(NULL)}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcretaindevice]{rtcRetainDevice},
\hyperref[rtcreleasedevice]{rtcReleaseDevice},
\hyperref[rtcnewsycldevice]{rtcNewSYCLDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcNewSYCLDevice}\label{rtcnewsycldevice}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcNewSYCLDevice }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ creates a }\KeywordTok{new}\NormalTok{ device to be used with SYCL}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCDevice rtcNewSYCLDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{context context}\OperatorTok{,} \AttributeTok{const} \DataTypeTok{char}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ config}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function creates a new device to be used with SYCL for GPU
rendering and returns a handle to this device. The device object is
reference counted with an initial reference count of 1. The handle can
get released using the \texttt{rtcReleaseDevice} API call.

The passed SYCL context (\texttt{context} argument) is used to allocate
GPU data, thus only devices contained inside this context can be used
for rendering. By default the GPU data is allocated on the first GPU
device of the context, but this behavior can get changed with the
\hyperref[rtcsetdevicesycldevice]{rtcSetDeviceSYCLDevice} function.

The device object acts as a class factory for all other object types.
All objects created from the device (like scenes, geometries, etc.) hold
a reference to the device, thus the device will not be destroyed unless
these objects are destroyed first.

Objects are only compatible if they belong to the same device, e.g it is
not allowed to create a geometry in one device and attach it to a scene
created with a different device.

For an overview of configurations that can get passed (\texttt{config}
argument) please see the \hyperref[rtcnewdevice]{rtcNewDevice} function
description.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On success returns a handle of the created device. On failure returns
\texttt{NULL} as device and sets a per-thread error code that can be
queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError(NULL)}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcretaindevice]{rtcRetainDevice},
\hyperref[rtcreleasedevice]{rtcReleaseDevice},
\hyperref[rtcnewdevice]{rtcNewDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcIsSYCLDeviceSupported}\label{rtcissycldevicesupported}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcIsSYCLDeviceSupported }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ checks }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ some SYCL device is supported by Embree}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ rtcIsSYCLDeviceSupported}\OperatorTok{(}\AttributeTok{const}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device sycl\_device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function can be used to check if some SYCL device
(\texttt{sycl\_device} argument) is supported by Embree.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

The function returns true if the SYCL device is supported by Embree and
false otherwise. On failure an error code is set that can get queried
using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsycldeviceselector]{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector}\label{rtcsycldeviceselector}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ SYCL device selector function to select}
\NormalTok{  devices supported by Embree}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ rtcSYCLDeviceSelector}\OperatorTok{(}\AttributeTok{const}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device sycl\_device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function checks if the passed SYCL device (\texttt{sycl\_device}
arguments) is supported by Embree or not. This function can be used
directly to select some supported SYCL device by using it as SYCL device
selector function. For instance, the following code sequence selects an
Embree supported SYCL device and creates an Embree device from it:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device sycl\_device}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{rtcSYCLDeviceSelector}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{queue sycl\_queue}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sycl\_device}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sycl\_device}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{RTCDevice device }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rtcNewSYCLDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sycl\_context}\OperatorTok{,}\KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

The function returns -1 if the SYCL device is supported by Embree and 1
otherwise. On failure an error code is set that can get queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcissycldevicesupported]{rtcIsSYCLDeviceSupported}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSetDeviceSYCLDevice}\label{rtcsetdevicesycldevice}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSetDeviceSYCLDevice }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ sets the SYCL device to be used }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ memory allocations}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcSetDeviceSYCLDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{,} \AttributeTok{const}\NormalTok{ sycl}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{device sycl\_device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function sets the SYCL device (\texttt{sycl\_device} argument) to
be used to allocate GPU memory when using the specified Embree device
(\texttt{device} argument). This SYCL device must be one of the SYCL
devices contained inside the SYCL context used to create the Embree
device.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can get queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewsycldevice]{rtcNewSYCLDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcRetainDevice}\label{rtcretaindevice}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcRetainDevice }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ increments the device reference count}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcRetainDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Device objects are reference counted. The \texttt{rtcRetainDevice}
function increments the reference count of the passed device object
(\texttt{device} argument). This function together with
\texttt{rtcReleaseDevice} allows to use the internal reference counting
in a C++ wrapper class to manage the ownership of the object.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewdevice]{rtcNewDevice},
\hyperref[rtcreleasedevice]{rtcReleaseDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcReleaseDevice}\label{rtcreleasedevice}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcReleaseDevice }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ decrements the device reference count}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcReleaseDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Device objects are reference counted. The \texttt{rtcReleaseDevice}
function decrements the reference count of the passed device object
(\texttt{device} argument). When the reference count falls to 0, the
device gets destroyed.

All objects created from the device (like scenes, geometries, etc.) hold
a reference to the device, thus the device will not get destroyed unless
these objects are destroyed first.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewdevice]{rtcNewDevice},
\hyperref[rtcretaindevice]{rtcRetainDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetDeviceProperty}\label{rtcgetdeviceproperty}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetDeviceProperty }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ queries properties of the device}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{ssize\_t}\NormalTok{ rtcGetDeviceProperty}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCDeviceProperty prop}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcGetDeviceProperty} function can be used to query
properties (\texttt{prop} argument) of a device object (\texttt{device}
argument). The returned property is an integer of type
\texttt{ssize\_t}.

Possible properties to query are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_VERSION}: Queries the combined version
  number (MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH) with two decimal digits per component. E.g.
  for Embree 2.8.3 the integer 208003 is returned.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_VERSION\_MAJOR}: Queries the major
  version number of Embree.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_VERSION\_MINOR}: Queries the minor
  version number of Embree.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_VERSION\_PATCH}: Queries the patch
  version number of Embree.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_NATIVE\_RAY4\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether the \texttt{rtcIntersect4} and \texttt{rtcOccluded4} functions
  preserve packet size and ray order when invoking callback functions.
  This is only the case if Embree is compiled with
  \texttt{EMBREE\_RAY\_PACKETS} and \texttt{SSE2} (or \texttt{SSE4.2})
  enabled, and if the machine it is running on supports \texttt{SSE2}
  (or \texttt{SSE4.2}).
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_NATIVE\_RAY8\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether the \texttt{rtcIntersect8} and \texttt{rtcOccluded8} functions
  preserve packet size and ray order when invoking callback functions.
  This is only the case if Embree is compiled with
  \texttt{EMBREE\_RAY\_PACKETS} and \texttt{AVX} (or \texttt{AVX2})
  enabled, and if the machine it is running on supports \texttt{AVX} (or
  \texttt{AVX2}).
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_NATIVE\_RAY16\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether the \texttt{rtcIntersect16} and \texttt{rtcOccluded16}
  functions preserve packet size and ray order when invoking callback
  functions. This is only the case if Embree is compiled with
  \texttt{EMBREE\_RAY\_PACKETS} and \texttt{AVX512} enabled, and if the
  machine it is running on supports \texttt{AVX512}.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_RAY\_MASK\_SUPPORTED}: Queries whether
  ray masks are supported. This is only the case if Embree is compiled
  with \texttt{EMBREE\_RAY\_MASK} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_ENABLED}: Queries
  whether back face culling is enabled. This is only the case if Embree
  is compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_BACKFACE\_CULLING} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_CURVES\_ENABLED}:
  Queries whether back face culling for curves is enabled. This is only
  the case if Embree is compiled with
  \texttt{EMBREE\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_CURVES} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_SPHERES\_ENABLED}:
  Queries whether back face culling for spheres is enabled. This is only
  the case if Embree is compiled with
  \texttt{EMBREE\_BACKFACE\_CULLING\_SPHERES} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_COMPACT\_POLYS\_ENABLED}: Queries
  whether compact polys is enabled. This is only the case if Embree is
  compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_COMPACT\_POLYS} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_FILTER\_FUNCTION\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether filter functions are supported, which is the case if Embree is
  compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_FILTER\_FUNCTION} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_IGNORE\_INVALID\_RAYS\_ENABLED}:
  Queries whether invalid rays are ignored, which is the case if Embree
  is compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_IGNORE\_INVALID\_RAYS} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_TRIANGLE\_GEOMETRY\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether triangles are supported, which is the case if Embree is
  compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_TRIANGLE} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_QUAD\_GEOMETRY\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether quads are supported, which is the case if Embree is compiled
  with \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_QUAD} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_SUBDIVISION\_GEOMETRY\_SUPPORTED}:
  Queries whether subdivision meshes are supported, which is the case if
  Embree is compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_SUBDIVISION}
  enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_CURVE\_GEOMETRY\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether curves are supported, which is the case if Embree is compiled
  with \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_CURVE} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_POINT\_GEOMETRY\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether points are supported, which is the case if Embree is compiled
  with \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_POINT} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_USER\_GEOMETRY\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether user geometries are supported, which is the case if Embree is
  compiled with \texttt{EMBREE\_GEOMETRY\_USER} enabled.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_TASKING\_SYSTEM}: Queries the tasking
  system Embree is compiled with. Possible return values are:

  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
  \setcounter{enumi}{-1}
  \tightlist
  \item
    internal tasking system
  \item
    Intel Threading Building Blocks (TBB)
  \item
    Parallel Patterns Library (PPL)
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_JOIN\_COMMIT\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} is supported. This is not the case
  when Embree is compiled with PPL or older versions of TBB.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_DEVICE\_PROPERTY\_PARALLEL\_COMMIT\_SUPPORTED}: Queries
  whether \texttt{rtcCommitScene} can get invoked from multiple TBB
  worker threads concurrently. This feature is only supported starting
  with TBB 2019 Update 9.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On success returns the value of the queried property. For properties
returning a boolean value, the return value 0 denotes \texttt{false} and
1 denotes \texttt{true}.

On failure zero is returned and an error code is set that can be queried
using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetDeviceError}\label{rtcgetdeviceerror}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetDeviceError }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the error code of the device}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCError rtcGetDeviceError}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Each thread has its own error code per device. If an error occurs when
calling an API function, this error code is set to the occurred error if
it stores no previous error. The \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError} function
reads and returns the currently stored error and clears the error code.
This assures that the returned error code is always the first error
occurred since the last invocation of \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

Possible error codes returned by \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError} are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_NONE}: No error occurred.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_UNKNOWN}: An unknown error has occurred.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_INVALID\_ARGUMENT}: An invalid argument was
  specified.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_INVALID\_OPERATION}: The operation is not allowed
  for the specified object.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_OUT\_OF\_MEMORY}: There is not enough memory left
  to complete the operation.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_UNSUPPORTED\_CPU}: The CPU is not supported as it
  does not support the lowest ISA Embree is compiled for.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_CANCELLED}: The operation got canceled by a memory
  monitor callback or progress monitor callback function.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_UNSUPPORTED\_GPU}: The specified SYCL GPU device
  is not supported.
\end{itemize}

When the device construction fails, \texttt{rtcNewDevice} returns
\texttt{NULL} as device. To detect the error code of a such a failed
device construction, pass \texttt{NULL} as device to the
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError} function. For all other invocations of
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}, a proper device pointer must be specified.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

Returns the error code for the device.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetdeviceerrorfunction]{rtcSetDeviceErrorFunction}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSetDeviceErrorFunction}\label{rtcsetdeviceerrorfunction}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSetDeviceErrorFunction }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ sets an error callback function }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ the device}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCErrorFunction}\OperatorTok{)(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCError code}\OperatorTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{const} \DataTypeTok{char}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ str}
\OperatorTok{);}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcSetDeviceErrorFunction}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCErrorFunction error}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Using the \texttt{rtcSetDeviceErrorFunction} call, it is possible to set
a callback function (\texttt{error} argument) with payload
(\texttt{userPtr} argument), which is called whenever an error occurs
for the specified device (\texttt{device} argument).

Only a single callback function can be registered per device, and
further invocations overwrite the previously set callback function.
Passing \texttt{NULL} as function pointer disables the registered
callback function.

When the registered callback function is invoked, it gets passed the
user-defined payload (\texttt{userPtr} argument as specified at
registration time), the error code (\texttt{code} argument) of the
occurred error, as well as a string (\texttt{str} argument) that further
describes the error.

The error code is also set if an error callback function is registered.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcgetdeviceerror]{rtcGetDeviceError}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSetDeviceMemoryMonitorFunction}\label{rtcsetdevicememorymonitorfunction}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSetDeviceMemoryMonitorFunction }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ registers a callback function}
\NormalTok{  to track memory consumption}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{bool} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCMemoryMonitorFunction}\OperatorTok{)(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{ssize\_t}\NormalTok{ bytes}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ post}
\OperatorTok{);}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcSetDeviceMemoryMonitorFunction}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCMemoryMonitorFunction memoryMonitor}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Using the \texttt{rtcSetDeviceMemoryMonitorFunction} call, it is
possible to register a callback function (\texttt{memoryMonitor}
argument) with payload (\texttt{userPtr} argument) for a device
(\texttt{device} argument), which is called whenever internal memory is
allocated or deallocated by objects of that device. Using this memory
monitor callback mechanism, the application can track the memory
consumption of an Embree device, and optionally terminate API calls that
consume too much memory.

Only a single callback function can be registered per device, and
further invocations overwrite the previously set callback function.
Passing \texttt{NULL} as function pointer disables the registered
callback function.

Once registered, the Embree device will invoke the memory monitor
callback function before or after it allocates or frees important memory
blocks. The callback function gets passed the payload as specified at
registration time (\texttt{userPtr} argument), the number of bytes
allocated or deallocated (\texttt{bytes} argument), and whether the
callback is invoked after the allocation or deallocation took place
(\texttt{post} argument). The callback function might get called from
multiple threads concurrently.

The application can track the current memory usage of the Embree device
by atomically accumulating the \texttt{bytes} input parameter provided
to the callback function. This parameter will be \textgreater0 for
allocations and \textless0 for deallocations.

Embree will continue its operation normally when returning \texttt{true}
from the callback function. If \texttt{false} is returned, Embree will
cancel the current operation with the
\texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_OUT\_OF\_MEMORY} error code. Issuing multiple cancel
requests from different threads is allowed. Canceling will only happen
when the callback was called for allocations (bytes \textgreater{} 0),
otherwise the cancel request will be ignored.

If a callback to cancel was invoked before the allocation happens
(\texttt{post\ ==\ false}), then the \texttt{bytes} parameter should not
be accumulated, as the allocation will never happen. If the callback to
cancel was invoked after the allocation happened
