(\texttt{post\ ==\ true}), then the \texttt{bytes} parameter should be
accumulated, as the allocation properly happened and a deallocation will
later free that data block.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewdevice]{rtcNewDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcNewScene}\label{rtcnewscene}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcNewScene }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ creates a }\KeywordTok{new}\NormalTok{ scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCScene rtcNewScene}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function creates a new scene bound to the specified device
(\texttt{device} argument), and returns a handle to this scene. The
scene object is reference counted with an initial reference count of 1.
The scene handle can be released using the \texttt{rtcReleaseScene} API
call.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On success a scene handle is returned. On failure \texttt{NULL} is
returned and an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcretainscene]{rtcRetainScene},
\hyperref[rtcreleasescene]{rtcReleaseScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetSceneDevice}\label{rtcgetscenedevice}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetSceneDevice }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the device the scene got created in}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCDevice rtcGetSceneDevice}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function returns the device object the scene got created in. The
returned handle own one additional reference to the device object, thus
you should need to call \texttt{rtcReleaseDevice} when the returned
handle is no longer required.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcreleasedevice]{rtcReleaseDevice}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcRetainScene}\label{rtcretainscene}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcRetainScene }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ increments the scene reference count}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcRetainScene}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Scene objects are reference counted. The \texttt{rtcRetainScene}
function increments the reference count of the passed scene object
(\texttt{scene} argument). This function together with
\texttt{rtcReleaseScene} allows to use the internal reference counting
in a C++ wrapper class to handle the ownership of the object.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewscene]{rtcNewScene},
\hyperref[rtcreleasescene]{rtcReleaseScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcReleaseScene}\label{rtcreleasescene}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcReleaseScene }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ decrements the scene reference count}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcReleaseScene}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Scene objects are reference counted. The \texttt{rtcReleaseScene}
function decrements the reference count of the passed scene object
(\texttt{scene} argument). When the reference count falls to 0, the
scene gets destroyed.

The scene holds a reference to all attached geometries, thus if the
scene gets destroyed, all geometries get detached and their reference
count decremented.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewscene]{rtcNewScene},
\hyperref[rtcretainscene]{rtcRetainScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcAttachGeometry}\label{rtcattachgeometry}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcAttachGeometry }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ attaches a geometry to the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ rtcAttachGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCGeometry geometry}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcAttachGeometry} function attaches a geometry
(\texttt{geometry} argument) to a scene (\texttt{scene} argument) and
assigns a geometry ID to that geometry. All geometries attached to a
scene are defined to be included inside the scene. A geometry can get
attached to multiple scenes. The geometry ID is unique for the scene,
and is used to identify the geometry when hit by a ray during ray
queries.

This function is thread-safe, thus multiple threads can attach
geometries to a scene in parallel.

The geometry IDs are assigned sequentially, starting from 0, as long as
no geometry got detached. If geometries got detached, the implementation
will reuse IDs in an implementation dependent way. Consequently
sequential assignment is no longer guaranteed, but a compact range of
IDs.

These rules allow the application to manage a dynamic array to
efficiently map from geometry IDs to its own geometry representation.
Alternatively, the application can also use per-geometry user data to
map to its geometry representation. See \texttt{rtcSetGeometryUserData}
and \texttt{rtcGetGeometryUserData} for more information.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetgeometryuserdata]{rtcSetGeometryUserData},
\hyperref[rtcgetgeometryuserdata]{rtcGetGeometryUserData}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcAttachGeometryByID}\label{rtcattachgeometrybyid}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcAttachGeometryByID }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ attaches a geometry to the scene}
  \KeywordTok{using}\NormalTok{ a specified geometry ID}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcAttachGeometryByID}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCGeometry geometry}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcAttachGeometryByID} function attaches a geometry
(\texttt{geometry} argument) to a scene (\texttt{scene} argument) and
assigns a user provided geometry ID (\texttt{geomID} argument) to that
geometry. All geometries attached to a scene are defined to be included
inside the scene. A geometry can get attached to multiple scenes. The
passed user-defined geometry ID is used to identify the geometry when
hit by a ray during ray queries. Using this function, it is possible to
share the same IDs to refer to geometries inside the application and
Embree.

This function is thread-safe, thus multiple threads can attach
geometries to a scene in parallel.

The user-provided geometry ID must be unused in the scene, otherwise the
creation of the geometry will fail. Further, the user-provided geometry
IDs should be compact, as Embree internally creates a vector which size
is equal to the largest geometry ID used. Creating very large geometry
IDs for small scenes would thus cause a memory consumption and
performance overhead.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcattachgeometry]{rtcAttachGeometry}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcDetachGeometry}\label{rtcdetachgeometry}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcDetachGeometry }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ detaches a geometry from the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcDetachGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function detaches a geometry identified by its geometry ID
(\texttt{geomID} argument) from a scene (\texttt{scene} argument). When
detached, the geometry is no longer contained in the scene.

This function is thread-safe, thus multiple threads can detach
geometries from a scene at the same time.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcattachgeometry]{rtcAttachGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcattachgeometrybyid]{rtcAttachGeometryByID}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetGeometry}\label{rtcgetgeometry}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetGeometry }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the geometry bound to}
\NormalTok{  the specified geometry ID}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry rtcGetGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcGetGeometry} function returns the geometry that is bound
to the specified geometry ID (\texttt{geomID} argument) for the
specified scene (\texttt{scene} argument). This function just looks up
the handle and does \emph{not} increment the reference count. If you
want to get ownership of the handle, you need to additionally call
\texttt{rtcRetainGeometry}.

This function is not thread safe and thus can be used during rendering.
However, it is generally recommended to store the geometry handle inside
the application's geometry representation and look up the geometry
handle from that representation directly.

If you need a thread safe version of this function please use
\hyperref[rtcgetgeometrythreadsafe]{rtcGetGeometryThreadSafe}.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcattachgeometry]{rtcAttachGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcattachgeometrybyid]{rtcAttachGeometryByID},
\hyperref[rtcgetgeometrythreadsafe]{rtcGetGeometryThreadSafe}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetGeometryThreadSafe}\label{rtcgetgeometrythreadsafe}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetGeometryThreadSafe }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the geometry bound to}
\NormalTok{  the specified geometry ID}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry rtcGetGeometryThreadSafe}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcGetGeometryThreadSafe} function returns the geometry that
is bound to the specified geometry ID (\texttt{geomID} argument) for the
specified scene (\texttt{scene} argument). This function just looks up
the handle and does \emph{not} increment the reference count. If you
want to get ownership of the handle, you need to additionally call
\texttt{rtcRetainGeometry}.

This function is thread safe and should NOT get used during rendering.
If you need a fast non-thread safe version during rendering please use
the \hyperref[rtcgetgeometry]{rtcGetGeometry} function.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcattachgeometry]{rtcAttachGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcattachgeometrybyid]{rtcAttachGeometryByID},
\hyperref[rtcgetgeometry]{rtcGetGeometry}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcCommitScene}\label{rtccommitscene}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcCommitScene }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ commits scene changes}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcCommitScene}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcCommitScene} function commits all changes for the
specified scene (\texttt{scene} argument). This internally triggers
building of a spatial acceleration structure for the scene using all
available worker threads. Ray queries can be performed only after
committing all scene changes.

If the application uses TBB 2019 Update 9 or later for parallelization
of rendering, lazy scene construction during rendering is supported by
\texttt{rtcCommitScene}. Therefore \texttt{rtcCommitScene} can get
called from multiple TBB worker threads concurrently for the same scene.
The \texttt{rtcCommitScene} function will then internally isolate the
scene construction using a tbb::isolated\_task\_group. The alternative
approach of using \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} which uses an
tbb:task\_arena internally, is not recommended due to it's high runtime
overhead.

If scene geometries get modified or attached or detached, the
\texttt{rtcCommitScene} call must be invoked before performing any
further ray queries for the scene; otherwise the effect of the ray query
is undefined. The modification of a geometry, committing the scene, and
tracing of rays must always happen sequentially, and never at the same
time. Any API call that sets a property of the scene or geometries
contained in the scene count as scene modification, e.g.~including
setting of intersection filter functions.

The kind of acceleration structure built can be influenced using scene
flags (see \texttt{rtcSetSceneFlags}), and the quality can be specified
using the \texttt{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality} function.

Embree silently ignores primitives during spatial acceleration structure
construction that would cause numerical issues, e.g.~primitives
containing NaNs, INFs, or values greater than 1.844E18f (as no
reasonable calculations can be performed with such values without
causing overflows).

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcjoincommitscene]{rtcJoinCommitScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcJoinCommitScene}\label{rtcjoincommitscene}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcJoinCommitScene }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ commits the scene from multiple threads}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcJoinCommitScene}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} function commits all changes for the
specified scene (\texttt{scene} argument). The scene commit internally
triggers building of a spatial acceleration structure for the scene. Ray
queries can be performed after scene changes got properly committed.

The \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} function can get called from multiple
user threads which will all cooperate in the build operation. All
threads calling into this function will return from
\texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} after the scene commit is finished. All
threads must consistently call \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} and not
\texttt{rtcCommitScene}.

In contrast to the \texttt{rtcCommitScene} function, the
\texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} function can be called from multiple user
threads, while the \texttt{rtcCommitScene} can only get called from
multiple TBB worker threads when used concurrently. For optimal
performance we strongly recommend using TBB inside the application
together with the \texttt{rtcCommitScene} function and to avoid using
the \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} function.

The \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} feature allows a flexible way to lazily
create hierarchies during rendering. A thread reaching a
not-yet-constructed sub-scene of a two-level scene can generate the
sub-scene geometry and call \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} on that just
generated scene. During construction, further threads reaching the
not-yet-built scene can join the build operation by also invoking
\texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene}. A thread that calls
\texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} after the build finishes will directly
return from the \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} call.

Multiple scene commit operations on different scenes can be running at
the same time, hence it is possible to commit many small scenes in
parallel, distributing the commits to many threads.

When using Embree with the Intel® Threading Building Blocks (which is
the default), threads that call \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} will join
the build operation, but other TBB worker threads might also participate
in the build. To avoid thread oversubscription, we recommend using TBB
also inside the application. Further, the join mode only works properly
starting with TBB v4.4 Update 1. For earlier TBB versions, threads that
call \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} to join a running build will just
trigger the build and wait for the build to finish. Further, old TBB
versions with \texttt{TBB\_INTERFACE\_VERSION\_MAJOR\ \textless{}\ 8} do
not support \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene}, and invoking this function will
result in an error.

When using Embree with the internal tasking system, only threads that
call \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} will perform the build operation, and
no additional worker threads will be scheduled.

When using Embree with the Parallel Patterns Library (PPL),
\texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} is not supported and calling that function
will result in an error.

To detect whether \texttt{rtcJoinCommitScene} is supported, use the
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceProperty} function.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtccommitscene]{rtcCommitScene},
\hyperref[rtcgetdeviceproperty]{rtcGetDeviceProperty}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSetSceneProgressMonitorFunction}\label{rtcsetsceneprogressmonitorfunction}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSetSceneProgressMonitorFunction }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ registers a callback}
\NormalTok{  to track build progress}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{bool} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCProgressMonitorFunction}\OperatorTok{)(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ptr}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{double}\NormalTok{ n}
\OperatorTok{);}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcSetSceneProgressMonitorFunction}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCProgressMonitorFunction progress}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Embree supports a progress monitor callback mechanism that can be used
to report progress of hierarchy build operations and to cancel build
operations.

The \texttt{rtcSetSceneProgressMonitorFunction} registers a progress
monitor callback function (\texttt{progress} argument) with payload
(\texttt{userPtr} argument) for the specified scene (\texttt{scene}
argument).

Only a single callback function can be registered per scene, and further
invocations overwrite the previously set callback function. Passing
\texttt{NULL} as function pointer disables the registered callback
function.

Once registered, Embree will invoke the callback function multiple times
during hierarchy build operations of the scene, by passing the payload
as set at registration time (\texttt{userPtr} argument), and a double in
the range \([0, 1]\) which estimates the progress of the operation
(\texttt{n} argument). The callback function might be called from
multiple threads concurrently.

When returning \texttt{true} from the callback function, Embree will
continue the build operation normally. When returning \texttt{false},
Embree will cancel the build operation with the
\texttt{RTC\_ERROR\_CANCELLED} error code. Issuing multiple cancel
requests for the same build operation is allowed.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewscene]{rtcNewScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality}\label{rtcsetscenebuildquality}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ sets the build quality }\ControlFlowTok{for}
\NormalTok{  the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcSetSceneBuildQuality}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildQuality quality}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality} function sets the build quality
(\texttt{quality} argument) for the specified scene (\texttt{scene}
argument). Possible values for the build quality are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_LOW}: Create lower quality data
  structures, e.g.~for dynamic scenes. A two-level spatial index
  structure is built when enabling this mode, which supports fast
  partial scene updates, and allows for setting a per-geometry build
  quality through the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuildQuality} function.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_MEDIUM}: Default build quality for most
  usages. Gives a good compromise between build and render performance.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_HIGH}: Create higher quality data
  structures for final-frame rendering. For certain geometry types this
  enables a spatial split BVH. When high quality mode is enabled, filter
  callbacks may be invoked multiple times for the same geometry.
\end{itemize}

Selecting a higher build quality results in better rendering performance
but slower scene commit times. The default build quality for a scene is
\texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_MEDIUM}.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrybuildquality]{rtcSetGeometryBuildQuality}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcSetSceneFlags}\label{rtcsetsceneflags}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcSetSceneFlags }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ sets the flags }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCSceneFlags}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_NONE                    }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_DYNAMIC                 }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{\textless{}\textless{}} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{),}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_COMPACT                 }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{\textless{}\textless{}} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{),}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_ROBUST                  }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{\textless{}\textless{}} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{),}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_FILTER\_FUNCTION\_IN\_ARGUMENTS }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{\textless{}\textless{}} \DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcSetSceneFlags}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCSceneFlags flags}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcSetSceneFlags} function sets the scene flags
(\texttt{flags} argument) for the specified scene (\texttt{scene}
argument). Possible scene flags are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_NONE}: No flags set.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_DYNAMIC}: Provides better build performance
  for dynamic scenes (but also higher memory consumption).
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_COMPACT}: Uses compact acceleration
  structures and avoids algorithms that consume much memory.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_ROBUST}: Uses acceleration structures that
  allow for robust traversal, and avoids optimizations that reduce
  arithmetic accuracy. This mode is typically used for avoiding
  artifacts caused by rays shooting through edges of neighboring
  primitives.
\item
  \texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_FILTER\_FUNCTION\_IN\_ARGUMENTS}: Enables
  scene support for filter functions passed as argument to the traversal
  functions. See Section
  \hyperref[rtcinitintersectarguments]{rtcInitIntersectArguments} and
  \hyperref[rtcinitoccludedarguments]{rtcInitOccludedArguments} for more
  details.
\end{itemize}

Multiple flags can be enabled using an \texttt{or} operation,
e.g.~\texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_COMPACT\ \textbar{}\ RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_ROBUST}.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcgetsceneflags]{rtcGetSceneFlags}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetSceneFlags}\label{rtcgetsceneflags}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetSceneFlags }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the flags of the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCSceneFlags rtcGetSceneFlags}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Queries the flags of a scene. This function can be useful when setting
individual flags, e.g.~to just set the robust mode without changing
other flags the following way:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTCSceneFlags flags }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rtcGetSceneFlags}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{scene}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcSetSceneFlags}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{scene}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_ROBUST }\OperatorTok{|}\NormalTok{ flags}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{RTC\_SCENE\_FLAG\_NONE} is returned and an error code
is set that can be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetsceneflags]{rtcSetSceneFlags}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetSceneBounds}\label{rtcgetscenebounds}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetSceneBounds }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the axis}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{aligned bounding box of the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCORE\_ALIGN}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ RTCBounds}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ lower\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ lower\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ lower\_z}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ align0}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ upper\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ upper\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ upper\_z}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ align1}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcGetSceneBounds}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBounds}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ bounds\_o}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcGetSceneBounds} function queries the axis-aligned
bounding box of the specified scene (\texttt{scene} argument) and stores
that bounding box to the provided destination pointer
(\texttt{bounds\_o} argument). The stored bounding box consists of lower
and upper bounds for the x, y, and z dimensions as specified by the
\texttt{RTCBounds} structure.

The provided destination pointer must be aligned to 16 bytes. The
function may be invoked only after committing the scene; otherwise the
result is undefined.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcgetscenelinearbounds]{rtcGetSceneLinearBounds},
\hyperref[rtccommitscene]{rtcCommitScene},
\hyperref[rtcjoincommitscene]{rtcJoinCommitScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcGetSceneLinearBounds}\label{rtcgetscenelinearbounds}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcGetSceneLinearBounds }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ returns the linear bounds of the scene}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCORE\_ALIGN}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ RTCLinearBounds}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  RTCBounds bounds0}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  RTCBounds bounds1}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcGetSceneLinearBounds}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCLinearBounds}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ bounds\_o}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcGetSceneLinearBounds} function queries the linear bounds
of the specified scene (\texttt{scene} argument) and stores them to the
provided destination pointer (\texttt{bounds\_o} argument). The stored
linear bounds consist of bounding boxes for time 0 (\texttt{bounds0}
member) and time 1 (\texttt{bounds1} member) as specified by the
\texttt{RTCLinearBounds} structure. Linearly interpolating these bounds
to a specific time \texttt{t} yields bounds for the geometry at that
time.

The provided destination pointer must be aligned to 16 bytes. The
function may be called only after committing the scene, otherwise the
result is undefined.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcgetscenebounds]{rtcGetSceneBounds},
\hyperref[rtccommitscene]{rtcCommitScene},
\hyperref[rtcjoincommitscene]{rtcJoinCommitScene}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcNewGeometry}\label{rtcnewgeometry}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ creates a }\KeywordTok{new}\NormalTok{ geometry object}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCGeometryType}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_LINEAR\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_HERMITE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BEZIER\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_HERMITE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CONE\_LINEAR\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_LINEAR\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BEZIER\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_HERMITE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SPHERE\_POINT}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_DISC\_POINT}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ORIENTED\_DISC\_POINT}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_USER}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_INSTANCE}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCGeometryType type}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Geometries are objects that represent an array of primitives of the same
type. The \texttt{rtcNewGeometry} function creates a new geometry of
specified type (\texttt{type} argument) bound to the specified device
(\texttt{device} argument) and returns a handle to this geometry. The
geometry object is reference counted with an initial reference count of
1. The geometry handle can be released using the
\texttt{rtcReleaseGeometry} API call.

Supported geometry types are triangle meshes
(\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE} type), quad meshes (triangle
pairs) (\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD} type), Catmull-Clark
subdivision surfaces (\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION} type),
curve geometries with different bases
(\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_LINEAR\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE},\\
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_HERMITE\_CURVE},\\
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BSPLINE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_HERMITE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CONE\_LINEAR\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_LINEAR\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BSPLINE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_HERMITE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE} types) grid
meshes (\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID}), point geometries
(\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SPHERE\_POINT},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_DISC\_POINT},
\texttt{RTC\_TYPE\_ORIENTED\_DISC\_POINT}), user-defined geometries
(\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_USER}), and instances
(\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_INSTANCE}).

The types \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}, and
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE} will treat the
curve as a sweep surface of a varying-radius circle swept tangentially
along the curve. The types
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}, and
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE} use ray-facing
ribbons as a faster-to-intersect approximation.

After construction, geometries are enabled by default and not attached
to any scene. Geometries can be disabled (\texttt{rtcDisableGeometry}
call), and enabled again (\texttt{rtcEnableGeometry} call). A geometry
can be attached to multiple scenes using the \texttt{rtcAttachGeometry}
call (or \texttt{rtcAttachGeometryByID} call), and detached using the
\texttt{rtcDetachGeometry} call. During attachment, a geometry ID is
assigned to the geometry (or assigned by the user when using the
\texttt{rtcAttachGeometryByID} call), which uniquely identifies the
geometry inside that scene. This identifier is returned when primitives
of the geometry are hit in later ray queries for the scene.

Geometries can also be modified, including their vertex and index
buffers. After modifying a buffer, \texttt{rtcUpdateGeometryBuffer} must
be called to notify that the buffer got modified.

The application can use the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryUserData} function to
set a user data pointer to its own geometry representation, and later
read out this pointer using the \texttt{rtcGetGeometryUserData}
function.

After setting up the geometry or modifying it,
\texttt{rtcCommitGeometry} must be called to finish the geometry setup.
After committing the geometry, vertex data interpolation can be
performed using the \texttt{rtcInterpolate} and \texttt{rtcInterpolateN}
functions.

A build quality can be specified for a geometry using the
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuildQuality} function, to balance between
acceleration structure build performance and ray query performance. The
build quality per geometry will be used if a two-level acceleration
structure is built internally, which is the case if the
\texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_LOW} is set as the scene build quality. See
Section \hyperref[rtcsetscenebuildquality]{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality} for
more details.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcenablegeometry]{rtcEnableGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcdisablegeometry]{rtcDisableGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcattachgeometry]{rtcAttachGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcattachgeometrybyid]{rtcAttachGeometryByID},
\hyperref[rtcupdategeometrybuffer]{rtcUpdateGeometryBuffer},
\hyperref[rtcsetgeometryuserdata]{rtcSetGeometryUserData},
\hyperref[rtcgetgeometryuserdata]{rtcGetGeometryUserData},
\hyperref[rtccommitgeometry]{rtcCommitGeometry},
\hyperref[rtcinterpolate]{rtcInterpolate},
\hyperref[rtcinterpolaten]{rtcInterpolateN},
\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrybuildquality]{rtcSetGeometryBuildQuality},
\hyperref[rtcsetscenebuildquality]{rtcSetSceneBuildQuality},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_triangle]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_quad]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_subdivision]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_curve]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CURVE},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_grid]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_point]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_POINT},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_user]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_USER},
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_instance]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_INSTANCE}

\pagebreak

\section{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE}\label{rtc_geometry_type_triangle}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ triangle geometry type}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry geometry }\OperatorTok{=}
\NormalTok{  rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Triangle meshes are created by passing
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_TRIANGLE} to the \texttt{rtcNewGeometry}
function call. The triangle indices can be specified by setting an index
buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_INDEX} type) and the triangle
vertices by setting a vertex buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX}
type). See \texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuffer} and
\texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer} for more details on how to set
buffers. The index buffer must contain an array of three 32-bit indices
per triangle (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT3} format) and the number of
primitives is inferred from the size of that buffer. The vertex buffer
must contain an array of single precision \texttt{x}, \texttt{y},
\texttt{z} floating point coordinates (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT3}
format), and the number of vertices are inferred from the size of that
buffer. The vertex buffer can be at most 16 GB large.

The parametrization of a triangle uses the first vertex \texttt{p0} as
base point, the vector \texttt{p1\ -\ p0} as u-direction and the vector
\texttt{p2\ -\ p0} as v-direction. Thus vertex attributes
\texttt{t0,t1,t2} can be linearly interpolated over the triangle the
following way:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{t\_uv }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{u}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{v}\OperatorTok{)*}\NormalTok{t0 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ u}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{t1 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ v}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{t2}
     \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ t0 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ u}\OperatorTok{*(}\NormalTok{t1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{t0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ v}\OperatorTok{*(}\NormalTok{t2}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{t0}\OperatorTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A triangle whose vertices are laid out counter-clockwise has its
geometry normal pointing upwards outside the front face, like
illustrated in the following picture:

!\href{images/triangle_uv.pdf}{}

For multi-segment motion blur, the number of time steps must be first
specified using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeStepCount} call. Then a
vertex buffer for each time step can be set using different buffer
slots, and all these buffers have to have the same stride and size.

Also see tutorial \hyperref[triangle-geometry]{Triangle Geometry} for an
example of how to create triangle meshes.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that be
get queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewgeometry]{rtcNewGeometry}

\pagebreak

\section{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD}\label{rtc_geometry_type_quad}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ quad geometry type}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry geometry }\OperatorTok{=}
\NormalTok{  rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Quad meshes are created by passing \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_QUAD} to
the \texttt{rtcNewGeometry} function call. The quad indices can be
specified by setting an index buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_INDEX}
type) and the quad vertices by setting a vertex buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX} type). See
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuffer} and \texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer}
for more details on how to set buffers. The index buffer contains an
array of four 32-bit indices per quad (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT4}
format), and the number of primitives is inferred from the size of that
buffer. The vertex buffer contains an array of single precision
\texttt{x}, \texttt{y}, \texttt{z} floating point coordinates
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT3} format), and the number of vertices is
inferred from the size of that buffer. The vertex buffer can be at most
16 GB large.

A quad is internally handled as a pair of two triangles
\texttt{v0,v1,v3} and \texttt{v2,v3,v1}, with the
\texttt{u\textquotesingle{}}/\texttt{v\textquotesingle{}} coordinates of
the second triangle corrected by \texttt{u\ =\ 1-u\textquotesingle{}}
and \texttt{v\ =\ 1-v\textquotesingle{}} to produce a quad
parametrization where \texttt{u} and \texttt{v} are in the range 0 to 1.
Thus the parametrization of a quad uses the first vertex \texttt{p0} as
base point, and the vector \texttt{p1\ -\ p0} as \texttt{u}-direction,
and \texttt{p3\ -\ p0} as v-direction. Thus vertex attributes
\texttt{t0,t1,t2,t3} can be bilinearly interpolated over the
quadrilateral the following way:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{t\_uv }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{v}\OperatorTok{)((}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{u}\OperatorTok{)*}\NormalTok{t0 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ u}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{t1}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ v}\OperatorTok{*((}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{u}\OperatorTok{)*}\NormalTok{t3 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ u}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{t2}\OperatorTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Mixed triangle/quad meshes are supported by encoding a triangle as a
quad, which can be achieved by replicating the last triangle vertex
(\texttt{v0,v1,v2} -\textgreater{} \texttt{v0,v1,v2,v2}). This way the
second triangle is a line (which can never get hit), and the
parametrization of the first triangle is compatible with the standard
triangle parametrization.

A quad whose vertices are laid out counter-clockwise has its geometry
normal pointing upwards outside the front face, like illustrated in the
following picture.

!\href{images/quad_uv.pdf}{}

For multi-segment motion blur, the number of time steps must be first
specified using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeStepCount} call. Then a
vertex buffer for each time step can be set using different buffer
slots, and all these buffers must have the same stride and size.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewgeometry]{rtcNewGeometry}

\pagebreak

\section{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID}\label{rtc_geometry_type_grid}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ grid geometry type}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry geometry }\OperatorTok{=}
\NormalTok{  rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Grid meshes are created by passing \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_GRID} to
the \texttt{rtcNewGeometry} function call, and contain an array of grid
primitives. This array of grids can be specified by setting up a grid
buffer (with \texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_GRID} type and
\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_GRID} format) and the grid mesh vertices by setting
a vertex buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX} type). See
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuffer} and \texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer}
for more details on how to set buffers. The number of grid primitives in
the grid mesh is inferred from the size of the grid buffer.

The vertex buffer contains an array of single precision \texttt{x},
\texttt{y}, \texttt{z} floating point coordinates
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT3} format), and the number of vertices is
inferred from the size of that buffer.

Each grid in the grid buffer is of the type \texttt{RTCGrid}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCGrid}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ startVertexID}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ stride}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{short}\NormalTok{ width}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{height}\OperatorTok{;} 
\OperatorTok{\};}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{RTCGrid} structure describes a 2D grid of vertices (with
respect to the vertex buffer of the grid mesh). The \texttt{width} and
\texttt{height} members specify the number of vertices in u and v
direction, e.g.~setting both \texttt{width} and \texttt{height} to 3
sets up a 3×3 vertex grid. The maximum allowed \texttt{width} and
\texttt{height} is 32767. The \texttt{startVertexID} specifies the ID of
the top-left vertex in the vertex grid, while the \texttt{stride}
parameter specifies a stride (in number of vertices) used to step to the
next row.

A vertex grid of dimensions \texttt{width} and \texttt{height} is
treated as a \texttt{(width-1)} x \texttt{(height-1)} grid of
\texttt{quads} (triangle-pairs), with the same shared edge handling as
for regular quad meshes. However, the \texttt{u}/\texttt{v} coordinates
have the uniform range \texttt{{[}0..1{]}} for an entire vertex grid.
The \texttt{u} direction follows the \texttt{width} of the grid while
the \texttt{v} direction the \texttt{height}.

For multi-segment motion blur, the number of time steps must be first
specified using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeStepCount} call. Then a
vertex buffer for each time step can be set using different buffer
slots, and all these buffers must have the same stride and size.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewgeometry]{rtcNewGeometry}

\pagebreak

\section{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION}\label{rtc_geometry_type_subdivision}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ subdivision geometry type}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCGeometry geometry }\OperatorTok{=}
\NormalTok{  rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Catmull-Clark subdivision meshes are supported, including support for
edge creases, vertex creases, holes, non-manifold geometry, and
face-varying interpolation. The number of vertices per face can be in
the range of 3 to 15 vertices (triangles, quadrilateral, pentagons,
etc).

Subdivision meshes are created by passing
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SUBDIVISION} to the \texttt{rtcNewGeometry}
function. Various buffers need to be set by the application to set up
the subdivision mesh. See \texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuffer} and
\texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer} for more details on how to set
buffers. The face buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_FACE} type and
\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT} format) contains the number of edges/indices
of each face (3 to 15), and the number of faces is inferred from the
size of this buffer. The index buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_INDEX}
type) contains multiple (3 to 15) 32-bit vertex indices
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT} format) for each face, and the number of
edges is inferred from the size of this buffer. The vertex buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX} type) stores an array of single
precision \texttt{x}, \texttt{y}, \texttt{z} floating point coordinates
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT3} format), and the number of vertices is
inferred from the size of this buffer.

Optionally, the application may set additional index buffers using
different buffer slots if multiple topologies are required for
face-varying interpolation. The standard vertex buffers
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX}) are always bound to the geometry
topology (topology 0) thus use \texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_INDEX} with
buffer slot 0. User vertex data interpolation may use different
topologies as described later.

Optionally, the application can set up the hole buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_HOLE}) which contains an array of 32-bit
indices (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT} format) of faces that should be
considered non-existing in all topologies. The number of holes is
inferred from the size of this buffer.

Optionally, the application can fill the level buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_LEVEL}) with a tessellation rate for each of
the edges of each face. This buffer must have the same size as the index
buffer. The tessellation level is a positive floating point value
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT} format) that specifies how many quads along
the edge should be generated during tessellation. If no level buffer is
specified, a level of 1 is used. The maximally supported edge level is
4096, and larger levels are clamped to that value. Note that edges may
be shared between (typically 2) faces. To guarantee a watertight
tessellation, the level of these shared edges should be identical. A
uniform tessellation rate for an entire subdivision mesh can be set by
using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTessellationRate} function. The
existence of a level buffer has precedence over the uniform tessellation
rate.

Optionally, the application can fill the sparse edge crease buffers to
make edges appear sharper. The edge crease index buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_EDGE\_CREASE\_INDEX}) contains an array of
pairs of 32-bit vertex indices (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT2} format) that
specify unoriented edges in the geometry topology. The edge crease
weight buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_EDGE\_CREASE\_WEIGHT}) stores
for each of these crease edges a positive floating point weight
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT} format). The number of edge creases is
inferred from the size of these buffers, which has to be identical. The
larger a weight, the sharper the edge. Specifying a weight of infinity
is supported and marks an edge as infinitely sharp. Storing an edge
multiple times with the same crease weight is allowed, but has lower
performance. Storing an edge multiple times with different crease
weights results in undefined behavior. For a stored edge (i,j), the
reverse direction edges (j,i) do not have to be stored, as both are
considered the same unoriented edge. Edge crease features are shared
between all topologies.

Optionally, the application can fill the sparse vertex crease buffers to
make vertices appear sharper. The vertex crease index buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX\_CREASE\_INDEX}), contains an array
of 32-bit vertex indices (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT} format) to specify
a set of vertices from the geometry topology. The vertex crease weight
buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX\_CREASE\_WEIGHT}) specifies
for each of these vertices a positive floating point weight
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT} format). The number of vertex creases is
inferred from the size of these buffers, and has to be identical. The
larger a weight, the sharper the vertex. Specifying a weight of infinity
is supported and makes the vertex infinitely sharp. Storing a vertex
multiple times with the same crease weight is allowed, but has lower
performance. Storing a vertex multiple times with different crease
weights results in undefined behavior. Vertex crease features are shared
between all topologies.

Subdivision modes can be used to force linear interpolation for parts of
the subdivision mesh; see \texttt{rtcSetGeometrySubdivisionMode} for
more details.

For multi-segment motion blur, the number of time steps must be first
specified using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeStepCount} call. Then a
vertex buffer for each time step can be set using different buffer
slots, and all these buffers have to have the same stride and size.

Also see tutorial \hyperref[subdivision-geometry]{Subdivision Geometry}
for an example of how to create subdivision surfaces.

\subsubsection{Parametrization}\label{parametrization}

The parametrization for subdivision faces is different for
quadrilaterals and non-quadrilateral faces.

The parametrization of a quadrilateral face uses the first vertex
\texttt{p0} as base point, and the vector \texttt{p1\ -\ p0} as
u-direction and \texttt{p3\ -\ p0} as v-direction.

The parametrization for all other face types (with number of vertices
not equal 4), have a special parametrization where the subpatch ID
\texttt{n} (of the \texttt{n}-th quadrilateral that would be obtained by
a single subdivision step) and the local hit location inside this
quadrilateral are encoded in the UV coordinates. The following code
extracts the sub-patch ID \texttt{i} and local UVs of this subpatch:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ l }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ floorf}\OperatorTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{U}\OperatorTok{);}
\DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ h }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ floorf}\OperatorTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{V}\OperatorTok{);}
\DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{h}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{l}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ u }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{2.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{fracf}\OperatorTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{U}\OperatorTok{){-}}\FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ v }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{2.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{fracf}\OperatorTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{V}\OperatorTok{){-}}\FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This encoding allows local subpatch UVs to be in the range
\texttt{{[}-0.5,1.5{[}} thus negative subpatch UVs can be passed to
\texttt{rtcInterpolate} to sample subpatches slightly out of bounds.
This can be useful to calculate derivatives using finite differences if
required. The encoding further has the property that one can just move
the value \texttt{u} (or \texttt{v}) on a subpatch by adding \texttt{du}
(or \texttt{dv}) to the special UV encoding as long as it does not fall
out of the \texttt{{[}-0.5,1.5{[}} range.

To smoothly interpolate vertex attributes over the subdivision surface
we recommend using the \texttt{rtcInterpolate} function, which will
apply the standard subdivision rules for interpolation and automatically
takes care of the special UV encoding for non-quadrilaterals.

\subsubsection{Face-Varying Data}\label{face-varying-data}

Face-varying interpolation is supported through multiple topologies per
subdivision mesh and binding such topologies to vertex attribute buffers
to interpolate. This way, texture coordinates may use a different
topology with additional boundaries to construct separate UV regions
inside one subdivision mesh.

Each such topology \texttt{i} has a separate index buffer (specified
using \texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_INDEX} with buffer slot \texttt{i}) and
separate subdivision mode that can be set using
\texttt{rtcSetGeometrySubdivisionMode}. A vertex attribute buffer
\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX\_ATTRIBUTE} bound to a buffer slot
\texttt{j} can be assigned to use a topology for interpolation using the
\texttt{rtcSetGeometryVertexAttributeTopology} call.

The face buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_FACE} type) is shared
between all topologies, which means that the \texttt{n}-th primitive
always has the same number of vertices (e.g.~being a triangle or a quad)
for each topology. However, the indices of the topologies themselves may
be different.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewgeometry]{rtcNewGeometry}

\pagebreak

\section{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CURVE}\label{rtc_geometry_type_curve}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_LINEAR\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  flat curve geometry with linear basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  flat curve geometry with cubic Bézier basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}} 
\NormalTok{  flat curve geometry with cubic B}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{spline basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_HERMITE\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}} 
\NormalTok{  flat curve geometry with cubic Hermite basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}} 
\NormalTok{  flat curve geometry with Catmull}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{Rom basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BEZIER\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  flat normal oriented curve geometry with cubic Bézier basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BSPLINE\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}} 
\NormalTok{  flat normal oriented curve geometry with cubic B}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{spline basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_HERMITE\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}} 
\NormalTok{  flat normal oriented curve geometry with cubic Hermite basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}} 
\NormalTok{  flat normal oriented curve geometry with Catmull}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{Rom basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CONE\_LINEAR\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  capped cone curve geometry with linear basis }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ discontinuous at edge boundaries}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_LINEAR\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  capped cone curve geometry with linear basis }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ spherical ending}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BEZIER\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  swept surface curve geometry with cubic Bézier basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BSPLINE\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  swept surface curve geometry with cubic B}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{spline basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_HERMITE\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  swept surface curve geometry with cubic Hermite basis}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  swept surface curve geometry with Catmull}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{Rom basis}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_LINEAR\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_HERMITE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BEZIER\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_HERMITE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CONE\_LINEAR\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_LINEAR\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BEZIER\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BSPLINE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_HERMITE\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{rtcNewGeometry}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{device}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

Curves with per vertex radii are supported with linear, cubic Bézier,
cubic B-spline, and cubic Hermite bases. Such curve geometries are
created by passing \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_LINEAR\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_HERMITE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_FLAT\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_FLAT\_BSPLINE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_FLAT\_HERMITE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_FLAT\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CONE\_LINEAR\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_LINEAR\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BEZIER\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_BSPLINE\_CURVE},
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_HERMITE\_CURVE}, or
\texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_CATMULL\_ROM\_CURVE} to the
\texttt{rtcNewGeometry} function. The curve indices can be specified
through an index buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_INDEX}) and the
curve vertices through a vertex buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_VERTEX}). For the Hermite basis a tangent
buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_TANGENT}), normal oriented curves a
normal buffer (\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_NORMAL}), and for normal
oriented Hermite curves a normal derivative buffer
(\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_DERIVATIVE}) has to get specified
additionally. See \texttt{rtcSetGeometryBuffer} and
\texttt{rtcSetSharedGeometryBuffer} for more details on how to set
buffers.

The index buffer contains an array of 32-bit indices
(\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_UINT} format), each pointing to the first control
vertex in the vertex buffer, but also to the first tangent in the
tangent buffer, and first normal in the normal buffer if these buffers
are present.

The vertex buffer stores each control vertex in the form of a single
precision position and radius stored in (\texttt{x}, \texttt{y},
\texttt{z}, \texttt{r}) order in memory (\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT4}
format). The number of vertices is inferred from the size of this
buffer. The radii may be smaller than zero but the interpolated radii
should always be greater or equal to zero. Similarly, the tangent buffer
stores the derivative of each control vertex (\texttt{x}, \texttt{y},
\texttt{z}, \texttt{r} order and \texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT4} format)
and the normal buffer stores a single precision normal per control
vertex (\texttt{x}, \texttt{y}, \texttt{z} order and
\texttt{RTC\_FORMAT\_FLOAT3} format).

\paragraph{Linear Basis}\label{linear-basis}

For the linear basis the indices point to the first of 2 consecutive
control points in the vertex buffer. The first control point is the
start and the second control point the end of the line segment. When
constructing hair strands in this basis, the end-point can be shared
with the start of the next line segment.

For the linear basis the user optionally can provide a flags buffer of
type \texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_FLAGS} which contains bytes that encode
if the left neighbor segment (\texttt{RTC\_CURVE\_FLAG\_NEIGHBOR\_LEFT}
flag) and/or right neighbor segment
(\texttt{RTC\_CURVE\_FLAG\_NEIGHBOR\_RIGHT} flags) exist (see
\hyperref[rtccurveflags]{RTCCurveFlags}). If this buffer is not set,
than the left/right neighbor bits are automatically calculated base on
the index buffer (left segment exists if segment(id-1)+1 == segment(id)
and right segment exists if segment(id+1)-1 == segment(id)).

A left neighbor segment is assumed to end at the start vertex of the
current segment, and to start at the previous vertex in the vertex
buffer. Similarly, the right neighbor segment is assumed to start at the
end vertex of the current segment, and to end at the next vertex in the
vertex buffer.

Only when the left and right bits are properly specified the current
segment can properly attach to the left and/or right neighbor, otherwise
the touching area may not get rendered properly.

\paragraph{Bézier Basis}\label{buxe9zier-basis}

For the cubic Bézier basis the indices point to the first of 4
consecutive control points in the vertex buffer. These control points
use the cubic Bézier basis, where the first control point represents the
start point of the curve, and the 4th control point the end point of the
curve. The Bézier basis is interpolating, thus the curve does go exactly
through the first and fourth control vertex.

\paragraph{B-spline Basis}\label{b-spline-basis}

For the cubic B-spline basis the indices point to the first of 4
consecutive control points in the vertex buffer. These control points
make up a cardinal cubic B-spline (implicit equidistant knot vector).
This basis is not interpolating, thus the curve does in general not go
through any of the control points directly. A big advantage of this
basis is that 3 control points can be shared for two continuous
neighboring curve segments, e.g.~the curves (p0,p1,p2,p3) and
(p1,p2,p3,p4) are C1 continuous. This feature makes this basis a good
choice to construct continuous multi-segment curves, as memory
consumption can be kept minimal.

\paragraph{Hermite Basis}\label{hermite-basis}

For the cubic Hermite basis the indices point to the first of 2
consecutive points in the vertex buffer, and the first of 2 consecutive
tangents in the tangent buffer. These two points and two tangents make
up a cubic Hermite curve. This basis is interpolating, thus does exactly
go through the first and second control point, and the first order
derivative at the begin and end matches exactly the value specified in
the tangent buffer. When connecting two segments continuously, the end
point and tangent of the previous segment can be shared. Different
versions of Catmull-Rom splines can be easily constructed using the
Hermite basis, by calculating a proper tangent buffer from the control
points.

\paragraph{Catmull-Rom Basis}\label{catmull-rom-basis}

For the Catmull-Rom basis the indices point to the first of 4
consecutive control points in the vertex buffer. This basis goes through
p1 and p2, with tangents (p2-p0)/2 and (p3-p1)/2.

\paragraph{Flat Curves}\label{flat-curves}

The \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_FLAT\_*} flat mode is a fast mode
designed to render distant hair. In this mode the curve is rendered as a
connected sequence of ray facing quads. Individual quads are considered
to have subpixel size, and zooming onto the curve might show geometric
artifacts. The number of quads to subdivide into can be specified
through the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTessellationRate} function. By default
the tessellation rate is 4.

\paragraph{Normal Oriented Curves}\label{normal-oriented-curves}

The \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_ORIENTED\_*} mode is a mode
designed to render blades of grass. In this mode a vertex spline has to
get specified as for the previous modes, but additionally a normal
spline is required. If the Hermite basis is used, the
\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_NORMAL} and
\texttt{RTC\_BUFFER\_TYPE\_NORMAL\_DERIVATIVE} buffers have both to be
set.

The curve is rendered as a flat band whose center approximately follows
the provided vertex spline, whose half width approximately follows the
provided radius spline, and whose normal orientation approximately
follows the provided normal spline.

To intersect the normal oriented curve, we perform a newton-raphson
style intersection of a ray with a tensor product surface of a linear
basis (perpendicular to the curve) and cubic Bézier basis (along the
curve). We use a guide curve and its derivatives to construct the
control points of that surface. The guide curve is defined by a sweep
surface defined by sweeping a line centered at the vertex spline
location along the curve. At each parameter value the half width of the
line matches the radius spline, and the direction matches the cross
product of the normal from the normal spline and tangent of the vertex
spline. Note that this construction does not work when the provided
normals are parallel to the curve direction. For this reason the
provided normals should best be kept as perpendicular to the curve
direction as possible. We further assume second order derivatives of the
center curve to be zero for this construction, as otherwise very large
curvatures occurring in corner cases, can thicken the constructed curve
significantly.

\paragraph{Round Curves}\label{round-curves}

In the \texttt{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ROUND\_*} round mode, a real
geometric surface is rendered for the curve, which is more expensive but
allows closeup views.

For the linear basis the round mode renders a cone that tangentially
touches a start-sphere and end-sphere. The start sphere is rendered when
no previous segments is indicated by the neighbor bits. The end sphere
is always rendered but parts that lie inside the next segment are
clipped away (if that next segment exists). This way a curve is closed
on both ends and the interior will render properly as long as only
neighboring segments penetrate into a segment. For this to work properly
it is important that the flags buffer is properly populated with
neighbor information.

For the cubic polynomial bases, the round mode renders a sweep surface
by sweeping a varying radius circle tangential along the curve. As a
limitation, the radius of the curve has to be smaller than the curvature
radius of the curve at each location on the curve.

The intersection with the curve segment stores the parametric hit
location along the curve segment as u-coordinate (range 0 to +1).

For flat curves, the v-coordinate is set to the normalized distance in
the range -1 to +1. For normal oriented curves the v-coordinate is in
the range 0 to 1. For the linear basis and in round mode the
v-coordinate is set to zero.

In flat mode, the geometry normal \texttt{Ng} is set to the tangent of
the curve at the hit location. In round mode and for normal oriented
curves, the geometry normal \texttt{Ng} is set to the non-normalized
geometric normal of the surface.

For multi-segment motion blur, the number of time steps must be first
specified using the \texttt{rtcSetGeometryTimeStepCount} call. Then a
vertex buffer for each time step can be set using different buffer
slots, and all these buffers must have the same stride and size. For the
Hermite basis also a tangent buffer has to be set for each time step and
for normal oriented curves a normal buffer has to get specified for each
time step.

Also see tutorials \hyperref[hair]{Hair} and
\hyperref[buxe9zier-curves]{Curves} for examples of how to create and
use curve geometries.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure \texttt{NULL} is returned and an error code is set that can
be queried using \texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewgeometry]{rtcNewGeometry},
\hyperref[rtccurveflags]{RTCCurveFlags}

\pagebreak

\section{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_POINT}\label{rtc_geometry_type_point}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_SPHERE\_POINT }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  point geometry spheres}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_DISC\_POINT }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  point geometry with ray}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{oriented discs}

\NormalTok{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_ORIENTED\_DISC\_POINT }\OperatorTok{{-}}
\NormalTok{  point geometry with normal}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{oriented discs}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}
