\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect4/8/16} functions forward the traversal
of a transformed ray packet (\texttt{ray} argument) into a scene
(\texttt{scene} argument) from a user geometry callback. The function
can only get invoked from a user geometry callback for a ray traversal
initiated with the \texttt{rtcIntersect4/8/16} function. The callback
arguments structure of the callback invokation has to get passed to the
ray forwarding (\texttt{args} argument). The user geometry callback
should instantly terminate after invoking the
\texttt{rtcForwardIntersect4/8/16} function.

Only the ray origin and ray direction members of the ray argument are
used for forwarding, all additional ray properties are inherited from
the initial ray traversal invokation of \texttt{rtcIntersect4/8/16}.

The implementation of the \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect4/8/16} function
recursively continues the ray traversal into the specified scene and
pushes the provided instance ID (\texttt{instID} argument) to the
instance ID stack. Hit information is updated into the ray hit structure
passed to the original \texttt{rtcIntersect4/8/16} invokation.

This function can get used to implement user defined instancing using
user geometries, e.g.~by transforming the ray in a special way, and/or
selecting between different scenes to instantiate.

When using Embree on the CPU it is possible to recursively invoke
\texttt{rtcIntersect4/8/16} directly from a user geometry callback.
However, when SYCL is used, recursively tracing rays is not directly
supported, and the \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect4/8/16} function must be
used.

For \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect4} the ray packet must be aligned to 16
bytes, for \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect8} the alignment must be 32 bytes,
and for \texttt{rtcForwardIntersect16} the alignment must be 64 bytes.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

For performance reasons this function does not do any error checks, thus
will not set any error flags on failure.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcintersect4816]{rtcIntersect4/8/16}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcForwardOccluded4/8/16}\label{rtcforwardoccluded4816}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcForwardOccluded4}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{8}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{16} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ forwards a ray packet to }\KeywordTok{new}\NormalTok{ scene}
\NormalTok{  from user geometry callback}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcForwardOccluded4}\OperatorTok{(}
  \DataTypeTok{void} \DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ valid}\OperatorTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCOccludedFunctionNArguments}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ args}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCRay4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ray}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ instID}
\OperatorTok{);}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcForwardOccluded4}\OperatorTok{(}
  \DataTypeTok{void} \DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ valid}\OperatorTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCOccludedFunctionNArguments}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ args}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCRay4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ray}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ instID}
\OperatorTok{);}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcForwardOccluded16}\OperatorTok{(}
  \DataTypeTok{void} \DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ valid}\OperatorTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCOccludedFunctionNArguments}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ args}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCRay16}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ray}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ instID}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded4/8/16} functions forward the traversal of
a transformed ray packet (\texttt{ray} argument) into a scene
(\texttt{scene} argument) from a user geometry callback. The function
can only get invoked from a user geometry callback for a ray traversal
initiated with the \texttt{rtcOccluded4/8/16} function. The callback
arguments structure of the callback invokation has to get passed to the
ray forwarding (\texttt{args} argument). The user geometry callback
should instantly terminate after invoking the
\texttt{rtcForwardOccluded4/8/16} function.

Only the ray origin and ray direction members of the ray argument are
used for forwarding, all additional ray properties are inherited from
the initial ray traversal invokation of \texttt{rtcOccluded4/8/16}.

The implementation of the \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded4/8/16} function
recursively continues the ray traversal into the specified scene and
pushes the provided instance ID (\texttt{instID} argument) to the
instance ID stack. Hit information is updated into the ray structure
passed to the original \texttt{rtcOccluded4/8/16} invokation.

This function can get used to implement user defined instancing using
user geometries, e.g.~by transforming the ray in a special way, and/or
selecting between different scenes to instantiate.

When using Embree on the CPU it is possible to recursively invoke
\texttt{rtcOccluded4/8/16} directly from a user geometry callback.
However, when SYCL is used, recursively tracing rays is not directly
supported, and the \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded4/8/16} function must be
used.

For \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded4} the ray packet must be aligned to 16
bytes, for \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded8} the alignment must be 32 bytes,
and for \texttt{rtcForwardOccluded16} the alignment must be 64 bytes.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

For performance reasons this function does not do any error checks, thus
will not set any error flags on failure.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcoccluded4816]{rtcOccluded4/8/16}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcInitPointQueryContext}\label{rtcinitpointquerycontext}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcInitPointQueryContext }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ initializes the context information }\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{e}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{g}\OperatorTok{.}
\NormalTok{  stack of }\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{multilevel}\OperatorTok{{-})}\NormalTok{instance transformations}\OperatorTok{)} \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ point queries}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTC\_ALIGN}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ RTCPointQueryContext}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// accumulated 4x4 column major matrices from world to instance space.}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ world2inst}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{RTC\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT}\OperatorTok{][}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{];}
  
  \CommentTok{// accumulated 4x4 column major matrices from instance to world space.}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ inst2world}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{RTC\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT}\OperatorTok{][}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{];}

  \CommentTok{// instance ids.}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ instID}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{RTC\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT}\OperatorTok{];}
  
  \CommentTok{// number of instances currently on the stack.}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ instStackSize}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcInitPointQueryContext}\OperatorTok{(}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCPointQueryContext}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ context}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

A stack (\texttt{RTCPointQueryContext} type) which stores the IDs and
instance transformations during a BVH traversal for a point query. The
transformations are assumed to be affine transformations (3×3 matrix
plus translation) and therefore the last column is ignored (see
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_instance]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_INSTANCE} for
details).

The \texttt{rtcInitPointContext} function initializes the context to
default values and should be called for initialization.

The context will be passed as an argument to the point query callback
function (see
\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrypointqueryfunction]{rtcSetGeometryPointQueryFunction})
and should be used to pass instance information down the instancing
chain for user defined instancing (see tutorial {[}ClosestPoint{]} for a
reference implementation of point queries with user defined instancing).

The context is an necessary argument to
\hyperref[rtcpointquery]{rtcPointQuery} and Embree internally uses the
topmost instance transformation of the stack to transform the point
query into instance space.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

No error code is set by this function.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcpointquery]{rtcPointQuery},
\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrypointqueryfunction]{rtcSetGeometryPointQueryFunction}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcPointQuery}\label{rtcpointquery}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcPointQuery }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ traverses the BVH with a point query object}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTC\_ALIGN}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{16}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ RTCPointQuery}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// location of the query}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ x}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ y}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ z}\OperatorTok{;}

  \CommentTok{// radius and time of the query}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ radius}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ time}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcPointQuery}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCScene scene}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCPointQuery}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ query}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCPointQueryContext}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ context}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCPointQueryFunction}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ queryFunc}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcPointQuery} function traverses the BVH using a
\texttt{RTCPointQuery} object (\texttt{query} argument) and calls a user
defined callback function (e.g \texttt{queryFunc} argument) for each
primitive of the scene (\texttt{scene} argument) that intersects the
query domain.

The user has to initialize the query location (\texttt{x}, \texttt{y}
and \texttt{z} member) and query radius in the range \([0, \infty]\). If
the scene contains motion blur geometries, also the query time
(\texttt{time} member) must be initialized to a value in the range
\([0, 1]\).

Further, a \texttt{RTCPointQueryContext} (\texttt{context} argument)
must be created and initialized. It contains ID and transformation
information of the instancing hierarchy if (multilevel-)instancing is
used. See \hyperref[rtcinitpointquerycontext]{rtcInitPointQueryContext}
for further information.

For every primitive that intersects the query domain, the callback
function (\texttt{queryFunc} argument) is called, in which distance
computations to the primitive can be implemented. The user will be
provided with the primID and geomID of the according primitive, however,
the geometry information (e.g.~triangle index and vertex data) has to be
determined manually. The \texttt{userPtr} argument can be used to input
geometry data of the scene or output results of the point query
(e.g.~closest point currently found on surface geometry (see tutorial
{[}ClosestPoint{]})).

The parameter \texttt{queryFunc} is optional and can be NULL, in which
case the callback function is not invoked. However, a callback function
can still get attached to a specific \texttt{RTCGeometry} object using
\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrypointqueryfunction]{rtcSetGeometryPointQueryFunction}.
If a callback function is attached to a geometry and (a potentially
different) callback function is passed as an argument to
\texttt{rtcPointQuery}, both functions are called for the primitives of
the according geometries.

The query radius can be decreased inside the callback function, which
allows to efficiently cull parts of the scene during BVH traversal.
Increasing the query radius and modifying time or location of the query
will result in undefined behaviour.

The callback function will be called for all primitives in a leaf node
of the BVH even if the primitive is outside the query domain, since
Embree does not gather geometry information of primitives internally.

Point queries can be used with (multilevel)-instancing. However, care
has to be taken when the instance transformation contains anisotropic
scaling or sheering. In these cases distance computations have to be
performed in world space to ensure correctness and the ellipsoidal query
domain (in instance space) will be approximated with its axis aligned
bounding box internally. Therefore, the callback function might be
invoked even for primitives in inner BVH nodes that do not intersect the
query domain. See
\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrypointqueryfunction]{rtcSetGeometryPointQueryFunction}
for details.

The point query structure must be aligned to 16 bytes.

\subsubsection{SUPPORTED PRIMITIVES}\label{supported-primitives}

Currently, all primitive types are supported by the point query API
except of points (see
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_point]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_POINT}), curves
(see \hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_curve]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_CURVE}) and
sudivision surfaces (see {[}RTC\_GEOMETRY\_SUBDIVISION{]}).

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

For performance reasons this function does not do any error checks, thus
will not set any error flags on failure.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrypointqueryfunction]{rtcSetGeometryPointQueryFunction},
\hyperref[rtcinitpointquerycontext]{rtcInitPointQueryContext}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcCollide}\label{rtccollide}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcCollide }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ intersects one BVH with another}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCCollision }\OperatorTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID0}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ primID0}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ primID1}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCCollideFunc}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTCCollision}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ collisions}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ num\_collisions}\OperatorTok{);}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcCollide }\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{    RTCScene hscene0}\OperatorTok{,} 
\NormalTok{    RTCScene hscene1}\OperatorTok{,} 
\NormalTok{    RTCCollideFunc callback}\OperatorTok{,} 
    \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcCollide} function intersects the BVH of \texttt{hscene0}
with the BVH of scene \texttt{hscene1} and calls a user defined callback
function (e.g \texttt{callback} argument) for each pair of intersecting
primitives between the two scenes. A user defined data pointer
(\texttt{userPtr} argument) can also be passed in.

For every pair of primitives that may intersect each other, the callback
function (\texttt{callback} argument) is called. The user will be
provided with the primID's and geomID's of multiple potentially
intersecting primitive pairs. Currently, only scene entirely composed of
user geometries are supported, thus the user is expected to implement a
primitive/primitive intersection to filter out false positives in the
callback function. The \texttt{userPtr} argument can be used to input
geometry data of the scene or output results of the intersection query.

\subsubsection{SUPPORTED PRIMITIVES}\label{supported-primitives}

Currently, the only supported type is the user geometry type (see
\hyperref[rtc_geometry_type_user]{RTC\_GEOMETRY\_TYPE\_USER}).

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcNewBVH}\label{rtcnewbvh}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcNewBVH }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ creates a }\KeywordTok{new}\NormalTok{ BVH object}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{RTCBVH rtcNewBVH}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCDevice device}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

This function creates a new BVH object and returns a handle to this BVH.
The BVH object is reference counted with an initial reference count of
1. The handle can be released using the \texttt{rtcReleaseBVH} API call.

The BVH object can be used to build a BVH in a user-specified format
over user-specified primitives. See the documentation of the
\texttt{rtcBuildBVH} call for more details.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcretainbvh]{rtcRetainBVH},
\hyperref[rtcreleasebvh]{rtcReleaseBVH},
\hyperref[rtcbuildbvh]{rtcBuildBVH}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcRetainBVH}\label{rtcretainbvh}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcRetainBVH }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ increments the BVH reference count}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcRetainBVH}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCBVH bvh}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

BVH objects are reference counted. The \texttt{rtcRetainBVH} function
increments the reference count of the passed BVH object (\texttt{bvh}
argument). This function together with \texttt{rtcReleaseBVH} allows to
use the internal reference counting in a C++ wrapper class to handle the
ownership of the object.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewbvh]{rtcNewBVH}, \hyperref[rtcreleasebvh]{rtcReleaseBVH}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcReleaseBVH}\label{rtcreleasebvh}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcReleaseBVH }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ decrements the BVH reference count}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcReleaseBVH}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{RTCBVH bvh}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

BVH objects are reference counted. The \texttt{rtcReleaseBVH} function
decrements the reference count of the passed BVH object (\texttt{bvh}
argument). When the reference count falls to 0, the BVH gets destroyed.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewbvh]{rtcNewBVH}, \hyperref[rtcretainbvh]{rtcRetainBVH}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcBuildBVH}\label{rtcbuildbvh}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcBuildBVH }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ builds a BVH}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}embree4/rtcore.h\textgreater{}}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTC\_ALIGN}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{32}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildPrimitive}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ lower\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ lower\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ lower\_z}\OperatorTok{;} 
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ geomID}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ upper\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ upper\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ upper\_z}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ primID}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCCreateNodeFunction}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCThreadLocalAllocator allocator}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ childCount}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCSetNodeChildrenFunction}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ nodePtr}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{ children}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ childCount}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCSetNodeBoundsFunction}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ nodePtr}\OperatorTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBounds}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{ bounds}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ childCount}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCCreateLeafFunction}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{  RTCThreadLocalAllocator allocator}\OperatorTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildPrimitive}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ primitives}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ primitiveCount}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{void} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCSplitPrimitiveFunction}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildPrimitive}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ primitive}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ dimension}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ position}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBounds}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ leftBounds}\OperatorTok{,}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBounds}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ rightBounds}\OperatorTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}
\OperatorTok{);}

\KeywordTok{typedef} \DataTypeTok{bool} \OperatorTok{(*}\NormalTok{RTCProgressMonitorFunction}\OperatorTok{)(}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{double}\NormalTok{ n}
\OperatorTok{);}

\KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildFlags}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_BUILD\_FLAG\_NONE}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{  RTC\_BUILD\_FLAG\_DYNAMIC}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildArguments}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ byteSize}\OperatorTok{;}

  \KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildQuality buildQuality}\OperatorTok{;}
  \KeywordTok{enum}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildFlags buildFlags}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ maxBranchingFactor}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ maxDepth}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ sahBlockSize}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ minLeafSize}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{unsigned} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ maxLeafSize}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ traversalCost}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ intersectionCost}\OperatorTok{;}

\NormalTok{  RTCBVH bvh}\OperatorTok{;}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildPrimitive}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ primitives}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ primitiveCount}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ primitiveArrayCapacity}\OperatorTok{;}
  
\NormalTok{  RTCCreateNodeFunction createNode}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  RTCSetNodeChildrenFunction setNodeChildren}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  RTCSetNodeBoundsFunction setNodeBounds}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  RTCCreateLeafFunction createLeaf}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  RTCSplitPrimitiveFunction splitPrimitive}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  RTCProgressMonitorFunction buildProgress}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ userPtr}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildArguments rtcDefaultBuildArguments}\OperatorTok{();}

\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ rtcBuildBVH}\OperatorTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{const} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCBuildArguments}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ args}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcBuildBVH} function can be used to build a BVH in a
user-defined format over arbitrary primitives. All arguments to the
function are provided through the \texttt{RTCBuildArguments} structure.
The first member of that structure must be set to the size of the
structure in bytes (\texttt{bytesSize} member) which allows future
extensions of the structure. It is recommended to initialize the build
arguments structure using the \texttt{rtcDefaultBuildArguments}
function.

The \texttt{rtcBuildBVH} function gets passed the BVH to build
(\texttt{bvh} member), the array of primitives (\texttt{primitives}
member), the capacity of that array (\texttt{primitiveArrayCapacity}
member), the number of primitives stored inside the array
(\texttt{primitiveCount} member), callback function pointers, and a
user-defined pointer (\texttt{userPtr} member) that is passed to all
callback functions when invoked. The \texttt{primitives} array can be
freed by the application after the BVH is built. All callback functions
are typically called from multiple threads, thus their implementation
must be thread-safe.

Four callback functions must be registered, which are invoked during
build to create BVH nodes (\texttt{createNode} member), to set the
pointers to all children (\texttt{setNodeChildren} member), to set the
bounding boxes of all children (\texttt{setNodeBounds} member), and to
create a leaf node (\texttt{createLeaf} member).

The function pointer to the primitive split function
(\texttt{splitPrimitive} member) may be \texttt{NULL}, however, then no
spatial splitting in high quality mode is possible. The function pointer
used to report the build progress (\texttt{buildProgress} member) is
optional and may also be \texttt{NULL}.

Further, some build settings are passed to configure the BVH build.
Using the build quality settings (\texttt{buildQuality} member), one can
select between a faster, low quality build which is good for dynamic
scenes, and a standard quality build for static scenes. One can also
specify the desired maximum branching factor of the BVH
(\texttt{maxBranchingFactor} member), the maximum depth the BVH should
have (\texttt{maxDepth} member), the block size for the SAH heuristic
(\texttt{sahBlockSize} member), the minimum and maximum leaf size
(\texttt{minLeafSize} and \texttt{maxLeafSize} member), and the
estimated costs of one traversal step and one primitive intersection
(\texttt{traversalCost} and \texttt{intersectionCost} members). When
enabling the \texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_FLAG\_DYNAMIC} build flags
(\texttt{buildFlags} member), re-build performance for dynamic scenes is
improved at the cost of higher memory requirements.

To spatially split primitives in high quality mode, the builder needs
extra space at the end of the build primitive array to store split
primitives. The total capacity of the build primitive array is passed
using the \texttt{primitiveArrayCapacity} member, and should be about
twice the number of primitives when using spatial splits.

The \texttt{RTCCreateNodeFunc} and \texttt{RTCCreateLeafFunc} callbacks
are passed a thread local allocator object that should be used for fast
allocation of nodes using the \texttt{rtcThreadLocalAlloc} function. We
strongly recommend using this allocation mechanism, as alternative
approaches like standard \texttt{malloc} can be over 10× slower. The
allocator object passed to the create callbacks may be used only inside
the current thread. Memory allocated using \texttt{rtcThreadLocalAlloc}
is automatically freed when the \texttt{RTCBVH} object is deleted. If
you use your own memory allocation scheme you have to free the memory
yourself when the \texttt{RTCBVH} object is no longer used.

The \texttt{RTCCreateNodeFunc} callback additionally gets the number of
children for this node in the range from 2 to
\texttt{maxBranchingFactor} (\texttt{childCount} argument).

The \texttt{RTCSetNodeChildFunc} callback function gets a pointer to the
node as input (\texttt{nodePtr} argument), an array of pointers to the
children (\texttt{childPtrs} argument), and the size of this array
(\texttt{childCount} argument).

The \texttt{RTCSetNodeBoundsFunc} callback function gets a pointer to
the node as input (\texttt{nodePtr} argument), an array of pointers to
the bounding boxes of the children (\texttt{bounds} argument), and the
size of this array (\texttt{childCount} argument).

The \texttt{RTCCreateLeafFunc} callback additionally gets an array of
primitives as input (\texttt{primitives} argument), and the size of this
array (\texttt{primitiveCount} argument). The callback should read the
\texttt{geomID} and \texttt{primID} members from the passed primitives
to construct the leaf.

The \texttt{RTCSplitPrimitiveFunc} callback is invoked in high quality
mode to split a primitive (\texttt{primitive} argument) at the specified
position (\texttt{position} argument) and dimension (\texttt{dimension}
argument). The callback should return bounds of the clipped left and
right parts of the primitive (\texttt{leftBounds} and
\texttt{rightBounds} arguments).

The \texttt{RTCProgressMonitorFunction} callback function is called with
the estimated completion rate \texttt{n} in the range \([0,1]\).
Returning \texttt{true} from the callback lets the build continue;
returning \texttt{false} cancels the build.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

On failure an error code is set that can be queried using
\texttt{rtcGetDeviceError}.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcnewbvh]{rtcNewBVH}

\pagebreak

\section{RTCQuaternionDecomposition}\label{rtcquaterniondecomposition}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTCQuaternionDecomposition }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ structure that represents a quaternion}
\NormalTok{  decomposition of an affine transformation}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCQuaternionDecomposition}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ scale\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ scale\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ scale\_z}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ skew\_xy}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ skew\_xz}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ skew\_yz}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ shift\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ shift\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ shift\_z}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ quaternion\_r}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ quaternion\_i}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ quaternion\_j}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ quaternion\_k}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ translation\_x}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ translation\_y}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ translation\_z}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\};}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The struct \texttt{RTCQuaternionDecomposition} represents an affine
transformation decomposed into three parts. An upper triangular
scaling/skew/shift matrix

\[
S = \left( \begin{array}{cccc}
scale_x & skew_{xy} & skew_{xz} & shift_x \\ 
0 & scale_y & skew_{yz} & shift_y \\ 
0 & 0 & scale_z & shift_z \\ 
0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 
\end{array} \right),
\]

a translation matrix

\[
T = \left( \begin{array}{cccc}
1 & 0 & 0 & translation_x \\ 
0 & 1 & 0 & translation_y \\ 
0 & 0 & 1 & translation_z \\ 
0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 
\end{array} \right),
\]

and a rotation matrix \(R\), represented as a quaternion

\(quaternion_r + quaternion_i \ \mathbf{i} + quaternion_j \ \mathbf{i} + quaternion_k \ \mathbf{k}\)

where \(\mathbf{i}\), \(\mathbf{j}\) \(\mathbf{k}\) are the imaginary
quaternion units. The passed quaternion will be normalized internally.

The affine transformation matrix corresponding to a
\texttt{RTCQuaternionDecomposition} is \(TRS\) and a point
\(p = (p_x, p_y, p_z, 1)^T\) will be transformed as
\[p' = T \ R \ S \ p.\]

The functions \texttt{rtcInitQuaternionDecomposition},
\texttt{rtcQuaternionDecompositionSetQuaternion},
\texttt{rtcQuaternionDecompositionSetScale},
\texttt{rtcQuaternionDecompositionSetSkew},
\texttt{rtcQuaternionDecompositionSetShift}, and
\texttt{rtcQuaternionDecompositionSetTranslation} allow to set the
fields of the structure more conveniently.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

No error code is set by this function.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrytransformquaternion]{rtcSetGeometryTransformQuaternion},
\hyperref[rtcinitquaterniondecomposition]{rtcInitQuaternionDecomposition}

\pagebreak

\section{rtcInitQuaternionDecomposition}\label{rtcinitquaterniondecomposition}

\subsubsection{NAME}\label{name}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rtcInitQuaternionDecomposition }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ initializes quaternion decomposition}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{SYNOPSIS}\label{synopsis}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ rtcInitQuaternionDecomposition}\OperatorTok{(}
  \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ RTCQuaternionDecomposition}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ qd}
\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{DESCRIPTION}\label{description}

The \texttt{rtcInitQuaternionDecomposition} function initializes a
\texttt{RTCQuaternionDecomposition} structure to represent an identity
transformation.

\subsubsection{EXIT STATUS}\label{exit-status}

No error code is set by this function.

\subsubsection{SEE ALSO}\label{see-also}

\hyperref[rtcsetgeometrytransformquaternion]{rtcSetGeometryTransformQuaternion},
\hyperref[rtcquaterniondecomposition]{RTCQuaternionDecomposition}

\pagebreak

\chapter{CPU Performance
Recommendations}\label{cpu-performance-recommendations}

\section{MXCSR control and status
register}\label{mxcsr-control-and-status-register}

It is strongly recommended to have the \texttt{Flush\ to\ Zero} and
\texttt{Denormals\ are\ Zero} mode of the MXCSR control and status
register enabled for each thread before calling the
\texttt{rtcIntersect}-type and \texttt{rtcOccluded}-type functions.
Otherwise, under some circumstances special handling of denormalized
floating point numbers can significantly reduce application and Embree
performance. When using Embree together with the Intel® Threading
Building Blocks, it is sufficient to execute the following code at the
beginning of the application main thread (before the creation of the
\texttt{tbb::task\_scheduler\_init} object):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}xmmintrin.h\textgreater{}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}pmmintrin.h\textgreater{}}
\OperatorTok{...}
\NormalTok{\_MM\_SET\_FLUSH\_ZERO\_MODE}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{\_MM\_FLUSH\_ZERO\_ON}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{\_MM\_SET\_DENORMALS\_ZERO\_MODE}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{\_MM\_DENORMALS\_ZERO\_ON}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If using a different tasking system, make sure each rendering thread has
the proper mode set.

\section{Thread Creation and Affinity
Settings}\label{thread-creation-and-affinity-settings}

Tasking systems like TBB create worker threads on demand, which will add
a runtime overhead for the very first \texttt{rtcCommitScene} call. In
case you want to benchmark the scene build time, you should start the
threads at application startup. You can let Embree start TBB threads by
passing \texttt{start\_threads=1} to the \texttt{cfg} parameter of
\texttt{rtcNewDevice}.

On machines with a high thread count (e.g.~dual-socket Xeon or Xeon Phi
machines), affinitizing TBB worker threads increases build and rendering
performance. You can let Embree affinitize TBB worker threads by passing
\texttt{set\_affinity=1} to the \texttt{cfg} parameter of
\texttt{rtcNewDevice}. By default, threads are not affinitized by Embree
with the exception of Xeon Phi Processors where they are affinitized by
default.

All Embree tutorials automatically start and affinitize TBB worker
threads by passing \texttt{start\_threads=1,set\_affinity=1} to
\texttt{rtcNewDevice}.

\section{Fast Coherent Rays}\label{fast-coherent-rays}

For getting the highest performance for highly coherent rays, e.g.
primary or hard shadow rays, it is recommended to use packets with
setting the \texttt{RTC\_RAY\_QUERY\_FLAG\_COHERENT} flag in the
\texttt{RTCIntersectArguments} struct passed to the
\texttt{rtcIntersect}/\texttt{rtcOccluded} calls. The rays inside each
packet should be grouped as coherent as possible.

\section{Huge Page Support}\label{huge-page-support}

It is recommended to use huge pages under Linux to increase rendering
performance. Embree supports 2MB huge pages under Windows, Linux, and
macOS. Under Linux huge page support is enabled by default, and under
Windows and macOS disabled by default. Huge page support can be enabled
in Embree by passing \texttt{hugepages=1} to \texttt{rtcNewDevice} or
disabled by passing \texttt{hugepages=0} to \texttt{rtcNewDevice}.

We recommend using 2MB huge pages with Embree under Linux as this
improves ray tracing performance by about 5-10\%. Under Windows using
huge pages requires the application to run in elevated mode which is a
security issue, thus likely not an option for most use cases. Under
macOS huge pages are rarely available as memory tends to get quickly
fragmented, thus we do not recommend using huge pages on macOS.

\subsection{Huge Pages under Linux}\label{huge-pages-under-linux}

Linux supports transparent huge pages and explicit huge pages. To enable
transparent huge page support under Linux, execute the following as
root:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{echo always }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{sys}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{kernel}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{mm}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{transparent\_hugepage}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{enabled}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When transparent huge pages are enabled, the kernel tries to merge 4KB
pages to 2MB pages when possible as a background job. Many Linux
distributions have transparent huge pages enabled by default. See the
following webpage for more information on
\href{https://www.kernel.org/doc/Documentation/vm/transhuge.txt}{transparent
huge pages under Linux}. In this mode each application, including your
rendering application based on Embree, will automatically tend to use
huge pages.

Using transparent huge pages, the transitioning from 4KB to 2MB pages
might take some time. For that reason Embree also supports allocating
2MB pages directly when a huge page pool is configured. Such a pool can
be configured by writing some number of huge pages to allocate to
\texttt{/proc/sys/vm/nr\_overcommit\_hugepages} as root user. E.g. to
configure 2GB of address space for huge page allocation, execute the
following as root:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{echo }\DecValTok{1000} \OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{proc}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{sys}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{vm}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{nr\_overcommit\_hugepages}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

See the following webpage for more information on
\href{https://www.kernel.org/doc/Documentation/vm/hugetlbpage.txt}{huge
pages under Linux}.

\subsection{Huge Pages under Windows}\label{huge-pages-under-windows}

To use huge pages under Windows, the current user must have the ``Lock
pages in memory'' (SeLockMemoryPrivilege) assigned. This can be
configured through the ``Local Security Policy'' application, by adding
a user to ``Local Policies'' -\textgreater{} ``User Rights Assignment''
-\textgreater{} ``Lock pages in memory''. You have to log out and in
again for this change to take effect.

Further, your application must be executed as an elevated process (``Run
as administrator'') and the ``SeLockMemoryPrivilege'' must be explicitly
enabled by your application. Example code on how to enable this
privilege can be found in the ``common/sys/alloc.cpp'' file of Embree.
Alternatively, Embree will try to enable this privilege when passing
\texttt{enable\_selockmemoryprivilege=1} to \texttt{rtcNewDevice}.
Further, huge pages should be enabled in Embree by passing
\texttt{hugepages=1} to \texttt{rtcNewDevice}.

When the system has been running for a while, physical memory gets
fragmented, which can slow down the allocation of huge pages
significantly under Windows.

\subsection{Huge Pages under macOS}\label{huge-pages-under-macos}

To use huge pages under macOS you have to pass \texttt{hugepages=1} to
\texttt{rtcNewDevice} to enable that feature in Embree.

When the system has been running for a while, physical memory gets
quickly fragmented, and causes huge page allocations to fail. For this
reason, huge pages are not very useful under macOS in practice.

\section{Avoid store-to-load forwarding issues with single
rays}\label{avoid-store-to-load-forwarding-issues-with-single-rays}

We recommend to use a single SSE store to set up the \texttt{org} and
\texttt{tnear} components, and a single SSE store to set up the
\texttt{dir} and \texttt{time} components of a single ray
(\texttt{RTCRay} type). Storing these values using scalar stores causes
a store-to-load forwarding penalty because Embree is reading these
components using SSE loads later on.

\pagebreak

\chapter{GPU Performance
Recommendations}\label{gpu-performance-recommendations}

\section{Low Code Complexity}\label{low-code-complexity}

As a general rule try to keep code complexity low, to avoid spill code
generation. To achieve this we recommend splitting your renderer into
separate kernels instead of using a single Uber kernel invokation.

Code can further get reduced by using SYCL specialization constants to
just enable rendering features required to render a given scene.

\section{Feature Flags}\label{feature-flags}

Use SYCL specialization constants and the feature flags (see section
\hyperref[rtcfeatureflags]{RTCFeatureFlags}) of the
\texttt{rtcIntersect1} and \texttt{rtcOccluded1} calls to JIT compile
minimal code. The passed feature flags should just contain features
required to render the current scene. If JIT compile times are an issue,
reduce the number of feature masks used and use JIT caching (see section
\hyperref[sycl-jit-caching]{SYCL JIT caching}).

\section{Inline Indirect Calls}\label{inline-indirect-calls}

Attaching user geometry and intersection filter callbacks to the
geometries of the scene is not supported in SYCL for performance
reasons.

Instead directly pass the user geometry and intersection filter callback
functions through the \texttt{RTCIntersectArguments} (and
\texttt{RTCOccludedArguments}) struct to \texttt{rtcIntersect1} (and
\texttt{rtcOccluded1}) API functions as in the following example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{RTC\_SYCL\_INDIRECTLY\_CALLABLE }\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ intersectionFilter}\OperatorTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{const}\NormalTok{ RTCFilterFunctionNArguments}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ args}
\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{} \OperatorTok{...} \OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{RTCIntersectArguments args}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{rtcInitIntersectArguments}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{args}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{args}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{filter }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ intersectionFilter}\OperatorTok{;}

\NormalTok{rtcIntersect1}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{scene}\OperatorTok{,\&}\NormalTok{ray}\OperatorTok{,\&}\NormalTok{args}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If the callback function is directly passed that way, the SYCL compiler
can inline the indirect call, which gives a huge performance benefit. Do
\emph{not} read a function pointer form some memory location and pass it
to \texttt{rtcIntersect1} (and \texttt{rtcOccluded1}) as this will also
prevent inlining.

\section{7 Bit Ray Mask}\label{bit-ray-mask}

Use just the lower 7 bits of the ray and geometry mask if possible, even
though Embree supports 32 bit ray masks for geometry masking. On the CPU
using any of the 32 bits yields the same performance, but the ray
tracing hardware only supports an 8 bit mask, thus Embree has to emulate
32 bit masking if used. For that reason the lower 7 mask bits are
hardware accelerated and fast, while the mask bits 7-31 require some
software intervention and using them reduces performance. To turn on 32
bit ray masks use the RTC\_FEATURE\_FLAG\_32\_BIT\_RAY\_MASK (see
section \hyperref[rtcfeatureflags]{RTCFeatureFlags}).

\section{Limit Motion Blur Motions}\label{limit-motion-blur-motions}

The motion blur implementation on SYCL has some limitations regarding
supported motion. Primitive motion should be maximally as large as a
small multiple of the primitive size, otherwise performance can degrade
a lot. If detailed geometry moves fast, best put the geometry into an
instance, and apply motion blur to the instance itself, which
efficiently allows larger motions. As a fallback, problematic scenes can
always still get rendered robustly on the CPU.

\section{Generic Pointers}\label{generic-pointers}

Embree uses standard C++ pointers in its implementation. SYCL might not
be able to detect the memory space these pointers refer to and has to
treat them as generic pointers which are not performing optimal. The
DPC++ compiler has advanced optimizations to infer the proper address
space to avoid usage of generic pointers.

However, if you still encounter the following warning during ahead of
time compilation of SYCL kernels, then loads from generic pointer are
present:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{warning}\OperatorTok{:}\NormalTok{ Adding XX occurrences of additional control flow due to presence}
\NormalTok{         of generic address space operations in function YYY}\OperatorTok{.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

To work around this issue we recommend:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Do not use local memory inside kernels that trace rays. In this case
  the DPC++ compiler knows that no local memory pointer can exist and
  will optimize generic loads. As this is typically the case for
  renderers, generic pointer will typically not cause issues.
\item
  Indirectly callable functions may still cause problems, even if your
  kernel does not use local memory. Thus best use SYCL pointers like
  sycl::global\_ptr and sycl::private\_ptr in indirectly callable
  functions to avoid generic address space usage.
\item
  You can also enforce usage of global pointers using the following
  DPC++ compile flags:
  \texttt{-cl-intel-force-global-mem-allocation\ \ \ -cl-intel-no-local-to-generic}.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Embree Tutorials}\label{embree-tutorials}

Embree comes with a set of tutorials aimed at helping users understand
how Embree can be used and extended. There is a very basic minimal that
can be compiled as both C and C++, which should get new users started
quickly. All other tutorials exist in an Intel® ISPC and C++ version to
demonstrate the two versions of the API. Look for files named
\texttt{tutorialname\_device.ispc} for the Intel® ISPC implementation of
the tutorial, and files named \texttt{tutorialname\_device.cpp} for the
single ray C++ version of the tutorial. To start the C++ version use the
\texttt{tutorialname} executables, to start the Intel® ISPC version use
the \texttt{tutorialname\_ispc} executables. All tutorials can print
available command line options using the \texttt{-\/-help} command line
parameter.

For all tutorials except minimal, you can select an initial camera using
the \texttt{-\/-vp} (camera position), \texttt{-\/-vi} (camera look-at
point), \texttt{-\/-vu} (camera up vector), and \texttt{-\/-fov}
(vertical field of view) command line parameters:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{triangle\_geometry }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{vp }\DecValTok{10} \DecValTok{10} \DecValTok{10} \OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{vi }\DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can select the initial window size using the \texttt{-\/-size}
command line parameter, or start the tutorials in full screen using the
\texttt{-\/-fullscreen} parameter:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{triangle\_geometry }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{size }\DecValTok{1024} \DecValTok{1024}
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{triangle\_geometry }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{fullscreen}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The initialization string for the Embree device (\texttt{rtcNewDevice}
call) can be passed to the ray tracing core through the
\texttt{-\/-rtcore} command line parameter, e.g.:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{triangle\_geometry }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{rtcore verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{threads}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The navigation in the interactive display mode follows the camera orbit
model, where the camera revolves around the current center of interest.
With the left mouse button you can rotate around the center of interest
(the point initially set with \texttt{-\/-vi}). Holding Control pressed
while clicking the left mouse button rotates the camera around its
location. You can also use the arrow keys for navigation.

You can use the following keys:

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[F1]
Default shading
\item[F2]
Gray EyeLight shading
\item[F3]
Traces occlusion rays only.
\item[F4]
UV Coordinate visualization
\item[F5]
Geometry normal visualization
\item[F6]
Geometry ID visualization
\item[F7]
Geometry ID and Primitive ID visualization
\item[F8]
Simple shading with 16 rays per pixel for benchmarking.
\item[F9]
Switches to render cost visualization. Pressing again reduces
brightness.
\item[F10]
Switches to render cost visualization. Pressing again increases
brightness.
\item[f]
Enters or leaves full screen mode.
\item[c]
Prints camera parameters.
\item[ESC]
Exits the tutorial.
\item[q]
Exits the tutorial.
\end{description}

\section{Minimal}\label{minimal}

This tutorial is designed to get new users started with Embree. It can
be compiled as both C and C++. It demonstrates how to initialize a
device and scene, and how to intersect rays with the scene. There is no
image output to keep the tutorial as simple as possible.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/minimal/minimal.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Triangle Geometry}\label{triangle-geometry}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/triangle_geometry/triangle_geometry_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/triangle_geometry.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates the creation of a static cube and ground
plane using triangle meshes. It also demonstrates the use of the
\texttt{rtcIntersect1} and \texttt{rtcOccluded1} functions to render
primary visibility and hard shadows. The cube sides are colored based on
the ID of the hit primitive.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/triangle_geometry/triangle_geometry_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Dynamic Scene}\label{dynamic-scene}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/dynamic_scene/dynamic_scene_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/dynamic_scene.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates the creation of a dynamic scene, consisting
of several deforming spheres. Half of the spheres use the
\texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_REFIT} geometry build quality, which allows
Embree to use a refitting strategy for these spheres, the other half
uses the \texttt{RTC\_BUILD\_QUALITY\_LOW} geometry build quality,
causing a high performance rebuild of their spatial data structure each
frame. The spheres are colored based on the ID of the hit sphere
geometry.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/dynamic_scene/dynamic_scene_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Multi Scene Geometry}\label{multi-scene-geometry}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/multiscene_geometry/multiscene_geometry_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/dynamic_scene.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates the creation of multiple scenes sharing the
same geometry objects. Here, three scenes are built. One with all the
dynamic spheres of the Dynamic Scene test and two others each with half.
The ground plane is shared by all three scenes. The space bar is used to
cycle the scene chosen for rendering.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/multiscene_geometry/multiscene_geometry_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{User Geometry}\label{user-geometry}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/user_geometry/user_geometry_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/user_geometry.jpg}}

This tutorial shows the use of user-defined geometry, to re-implement
instancing, and to add analytic spheres. A two-level scene is created,
with a triangle mesh as ground plane, and several user geometries that
instance other scenes with a small number of spheres of different kinds.
The spheres are colored using the instance ID and geometry ID of the hit
sphere, to demonstrate how the same geometry instanced in different ways
can be distinguished.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/user_geometry/user_geometry_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Viewer}\label{viewer}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/viewer/viewer_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/viewer.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates a simple OBJ viewer that traces primary
visibility rays only. A scene consisting of multiple meshes is created,
each mesh sharing the index and vertex buffer with the application. It
also demonstrates how to support additional per-vertex data, such as
shading normals.

You need to specify an OBJ file at the command line for this tutorial to
work:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{viewer }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{i model}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{obj}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/viewer/viewer_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Intersection Filter}\label{intersection-filter}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/intersection_filter/intersection_filter_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/intersection_filter.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates the use of filter callback functions to
efficiently implement transparent objects. The filter function used for
primary rays lets the ray pass through the geometry if it is entirely
transparent. Otherwise, the shading loop handles the transparency
properly, by potentially shooting secondary rays. The filter function
used for shadow rays accumulates the transparency of all surfaces along
the ray, and terminates traversal if an opaque occluder is hit.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/intersection_filter/intersection_filter_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Instanced Geometry}\label{instanced-geometry}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/instanced_geometry/instanced_geometry_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/instanced_geometry.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates the in-build instancing feature of Embree, by
instancing a number of other scenes built from triangulated spheres. The
spheres are again colored using the instance ID and geometry ID of the
hit sphere, to demonstrate how the same geometry instanced in different
ways can be distinguished.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/instanced_geometry/instanced_geometry_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Multi Level Instancing}\label{multi-level-instancing}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/multi_instanced_geometry/multi_instanced_geometry_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/multi_level_instancing.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates multi-level instancing, i.e., nesting
instances into instances. To enable the tutorial, set the compile-time
variable \texttt{EMBREE\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT} to a value other
than the default 1. This variable is available in the code as
\texttt{RTC\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT}.

The renderer uses a basic path tracing approach, and the image will
progressively refine over time. There are two levels of instances in
this scene: multiple instances of the same tree nest instances of a
twig. Intersections on up to \texttt{RTC\_MAX\_INSTANCE\_LEVEL\_COUNT}
nested levels of instances work out of the box. Users may obtain the
\emph{instance ID stack} for a given hitpoint from the \texttt{instID}
member. During shading, the instance ID stack is used to accumulate
normal transformation matrices for each hit. The tutorial visualizes
transformed normals as colors.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/multi_instanced_geometry/multi_instanced_geometry_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Path Tracer}\label{path-tracer}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/pathtracer/pathtracer_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/pathtracer.jpg}}

This tutorial is a simple path tracer, based on the viewer tutorial.

You need to specify an OBJ file and light source at the command line for
this tutorial to work:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{pathtracer }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{i model}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{obj }\OperatorTok{{-}{-}}\NormalTok{ambientlight }\DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

As example models we provide the ``Austrian Imperial Crown'' model by
\href{http://www.loramel.net}{Martin Lubich} and the ``Asian Dragon''
model from the
\href{http://graphics.stanford.edu/data/3Dscanrep/}{Stanford 3D Scanning
Repository}.

\href{https://github.com/embree/models/releases/download/release/crown.zip}{crown.zip}

\href{https://github.com/embree/models/releases/download/release/asian_dragon.zip}{asian\_dragon.zip}

To render these models execute the following:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{pathtracer }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{c crown}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{crown}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ecs}
\OperatorTok{./}\NormalTok{pathtracer }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{c asian\_dragon}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{asian\_dragon}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{ecs}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/pathtracer/pathtracer_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Motion Blur Geometry}\label{motion-blur-geometry}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/motion_blur_geometry/motion_blur_geometry_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/motion_blur_geometry.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates rendering of motion blur using the
multi-segment motion blur feature. Shown is motion blur of a triangle
mesh, quad mesh, subdivision surface, line segments, hair geometry,
Bézier curves, instantiated triangle mesh where the instance moves,
instantiated quad mesh where the instance and the quads move, and user
geometry.

The number of time steps used can be configured using the
\texttt{-\/-time-steps\ \textless{}int\textgreater{}} and
\texttt{-\/-time-steps2\ \textless{}int\textgreater{}} command line
parameters, and the geometry can be rendered at a specific time using
the the \texttt{-\/-time\ \textless{}float\textgreater{}} command line
parameter.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/motion_blur_geometry/motion_blur_geometry_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Quaternion Motion Blur}\label{quaternion-motion-blur}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/quaternion_motion_blur/quaternion_motion_blur_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/quaternion_motion_blur.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates rendering of motion blur using quaternion
interpolation. Shown is motion blur using spherical linear interpolation
of the rotational component of the instance transformation on the left
and simple linear interpolation of the instance transformation on the
right. The number of time steps can be modified as well.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/quaternion_motion_blur/quaternion_motion_blur_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/interpolation/interpolation_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/interpolation.jpg}}

data.

\section{Closest Point}\label{closest-point}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/closest_point/closest_point_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/closest_point.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates a use-case of the point query API. The scene
consists of a simple collection of objects that are instanced and for
several point in the scene (red points) the closest point on the
surfaces of the scene are computed (white points). The closest point
functionality is implemented for Embree internal and for user-defined
instancing. The tutorial also illustrates how to handle instance
transformations that are not similarity transforms.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/closest_point/closest_point_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/voronoi/voronoi_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/voronoi.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates how to implement nearest neighbour lookups
using the point query API. Several colored points are located on a plane
and the corresponding voroni regions are illustrated.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/voronoi/voronoi_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Collision Detection}\label{collision-detection-1}

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/collide/collide_device.cpp}{\includegraphics{images/collide.jpg}}

This tutorial demonstrates how to implement collision detection using
the collide API. A simple cloth solver is setup to collide with a
sphere.

The cloth can be reset with the \texttt{space} bar. The sim stepped once
with \texttt{n} and continuous simulation started and paused with
\texttt{p}.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/collide/collide_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{BVH Builder}\label{bvh-builder}

This tutorial demonstrates how to use the templated hierarchy builders
of Embree to build a bounding volume hierarchy with a user-defined
memory layout using a high-quality SAH builder using spatial splits, a
standard SAH builder, and a very fast Morton builder.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/bvh_builder/bvh_builder_device.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{BVH Access}\label{bvh-access}

This tutorial demonstrates how to access the internal triangle
acceleration structure build by Embree. Please be aware that the
internal Embree data structures might change between Embree updates.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/bvh_access/bvh_access.cpp}{Source
Code}

\section{Find Embree}\label{find-embree}

This tutorial demonstrates how to use the \texttt{FIND\_PACKAGE} CMake
feature to use an installed Embree. Under Linux and macOS the tutorial
finds the Embree installation automatically, under Windows the
\texttt{embree\_DIR} CMake variable must be set to the following folder
of the Embree installation:
\texttt{C:\textbackslash{}Program\ Files\textbackslash{}Intel\textbackslash{}Embree3}.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/find_embree/CMakeLists.txt}{Source
Code}

\section{Next Hit}\label{next-hit}

This tutorial demonstrates how to robustly enumerate all hits along the
ray using multiple ray queries and an intersection filter function. To
improve performance, the tutorial also supports collecting the next N
hits in a single ray query.

\href{https://github.com/embree/embree/blob/master/tutorials/next_hit/next_hit_device.cpp}{Source
Code}
